# Saltpeter, Tartar, and Air are the Principles of the Stone.

"...impregnates itself with the astral seed, with the air, when the artist only places it in its proper disposition. It is born of itself, after it has married and impregnated itself, and it takes on a new birth of its own accord, like the **Phoenix** from the ashes. Blessed be God from heaven, who created nature in **salt**."

### §. 12.

We are directed toward **salt** in all sayings <note>original: *Dictis*</note>, and here we also see that **saltpeter** must be present, because it is meant to make the earth fertile. This fertility refers to the animation of the air, which **tartar** <term>tartar: a potassium salt found in wine barrels; in alchemy, it represents a "fixed" earthy substance capable of being "calcined" or purified by fire</term> does not possess on its own. The preparation of the matter is then the entire labor; everything else happens of its own accord. God in nature does it, and thus God Himself does all that is good, and the devil with the **coal fire** <note>The "coal fire" refers to the harsh, external heat of a furnace, which many alchemists warned against, preferring the "natural" or "internal" heat of the chemical process</note> must step back when the good is being made. Likewise, it is the holy God alone who works the good within us, and we do nothing in the process except prepare ourselves so that He can work within us.

**Philosophical Epistle:** "In **Sulfur**," namely in the **tartar**, "is the metallic power that **coagulates** <term>coagulation: the process of turning a liquid or gas into a solid or semi-solid state</term> the **Mercury**," as the air. "From the wine an oiliness is drawn that burns." By "the wine," the tartar is indicated as the Sulfur; therefore, he says further regarding the oiliness of the wine: "This is equal to Sulfur," namely that the tartar is the Sulfur, "and is truly in great power of the metallic nature, which it has drawn to itself especially from the earth." The earth is likewise the tartar itself.

**Sendivogius:** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and philosopher known for his theories on the "food of life" found in the air</note> "The **salt-niter** <term>salt-niter: another term for saltpeter or potassium nitrate</term> of the earth is like a **calcined** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a powder or "ash," used to purify a substance of its "gross" or physical impurities</term> tartar, which attracts the air to itself with its dryness. The saltpeter of the earth has such a power to attract, for it was also air once, and has been united with the fatness of the earth."

It emphasizes that the alchemist's role is to prepare the "disposition" of the matter, while the actual transformation is a natural and divine process.</summary>

# Saltpeter, Tartar, and Air are the Principles of the Stone.

„In the earth,“ in the <term>tartar</term> <note>Potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking, used here as a symbol for the "fixed" or earthly principle</note>, „dwells the fire; she is cold and dry; her exterior is <term>fixed</term> <note>stable and resistant to heat</note>, the interior <term>volatile</term> <note>evaporates easily when heated</note>.“

The tartar is fixed and serves for the <term>fixing</term> <note>original: *Figiren*; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> of the air. But when it has been turned into a spiritual nature through the air, then its interior has been turned outward, and then it is called volatile. Therefore, the philosophers say that one should make the volatile, such as the air, fixed, and the fixed volatile.

Therefore, the earth has communion with the fire, which is its innermost part. It is also purified only through fire, as if through its innermost self.“ With the word "Hell," he indicates the tartar, for hell is also called <term>Tartarus</term> <note>a play on words between the chemical substance "tartar" and the Greek mythological abyss of Tartarus</note>, and tartar is also the earth; therefore, the hellish fire is in the tartar.

# Saltpeter, Tartar, and Air are the Principles of the Stone.

...one must take <term>tartar</term> <note>Potassium bitartrate, a salty crust that forms in wine barrels; in alchemy, it represents the "fixed" or earthly salt</note> for the Stone. The <term>Great Farmer</term> <note>original: *Der grosse Bauer*; a pseudonym for an alchemical author, likely Johannes de Monte-Snyder</note> also says on page 53: "It is hidden in the wine barrel." Tartar is also the Gold of the Sages; therefore <term>Sendivogius</term> <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a celebrated Polish alchemist and physician</note> says that the <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>alcohol or the volatile essence of wine</note> has a great harmony with gold, and so they indicate the materials of the Stone only very cautiously, discursively, and through parables. However, whoever holds to nature and her possibilities, and looks toward a living, active nature in air and salt—rather than falling back on dead minerals, metals, etc., which are used only as metaphors—has had enough instruction in such philosophical books.

An <term>Anonymous Author</term> <note>original: *Anonimus*</note> states: "Philosophical gold is pure <term>sulphur</term> <note>In alchemy, 'sulphur' refers to the active, combustible, or 'soul' principle of a substance</note>," that is, tartar. "It is the true gold-sulphur, and in it lies the drying fire and the <term>fixing power</term> <note>the ability to make a volatile substance stable under heat</note>. It is the solar gold, the true 'Son of the Sun,' and is the true artist of nature, through whom the sympathetic forces manifest themselves, and through whom all <term>attractions</term> <note>original: *Attractiones*</note> occur. It is known that from gold," meaning from tartar, "a <term>gunpowder</term> <note>original: *Schießpulver*</note> may be made which strikes downward," because the tartar binds the air downward, "for it is of the nature of gold. But the gunpowder made from saltpeter drives upward, for saltpeter is mercurial," that is, the saltpeter dissolves the tartar so that it can pull upward toward the air. "Therefore, one must well understand the rapport, <term>sympathy, and antipathy</term> <note>the natural attractions and repulsions between different substances</note> that things have toward one another." Here, the two parts—saltpeter and tartar—are quite beautifully indicated.

# Saltpeter, Tartar, and Air are the Principles of the Stone.

...is from the stars, which we breathe every moment. Third-
ly, elemental gold, which is the salt, which is the purest
and most fixed part of the elements, and of all things com-
posed from them. All physical things have this gold within them.
These latter two types of gold alone are contained in our Stone,
and the elemental gold attracts the astral gold <note>The "astral gold" refers to the vital, life-giving spirit or energy found in the atmosphere, while "elemental gold" refers to the essential salt found within matter</note>,
and both become a <term>tincture</term> <note>A potent alchemical substance capable of transforming or "dyeing" other substances into a higher state</note>. The single thing
that is the subject of our Stone has no equal in the
world, and is of little value, and can be obtained at
small cost. The practice is the most difficult thing in the
entire world throughout the entire work, not in terms of the
labor itself, but in terms of the difficulty of learning
it from books, for which a deep knowledge of nature,
and a special grace and gift from God, is
required.

Likewise: "Our art does not occur in many things, but
only in body and spirit. The dissolution of the spirit," as the power
within the salt, "occurs only in its own blood," namely in air.

mere salt alone. Yet the salt is of no use unless
its innermost part has been turned outward, for
it is the spirit alone that gives power and life;
the mere body can do nothing toward it."

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the writings of Basil Valentine, specifically expanding on the previous discussion of how fire purifies elements into ash to reveal their essential salt. # Saltpeter, Tartar, and Air are the Principles of the Stone.

These speeches generally point us toward Salt; because in the burning of things, the salt remains behind in the ash. He specifically mentions the <term>Tartar</term> <note>Also known as "cream of tartar," this is the potassium bitartrate crystals that settle in wine casks; alchemists viewed it as a potent source of "philosophical salt"</note> by name. But the mere physical body of the salt does not achieve the work; rather, the Spirit of the World <note>The *Anima Mundi*, or the vital life force believed to permeate the universe</note> must be brought into it, which then elevates the salt itself into a spiritual nature. How beautifully all of this illustrates the renewal of all things, as we are enlivened and acted upon by the Holy Spirit of God. There we attain the white garments—the white color of the salt—which emerges once again from the ashes of our previous corruption. There the Phoenix, which existed with God's blessing at the beginning of the world, will bring forth its young once more, and through the Spirit of Life from God, we will be released from the bonds in which we previously lay bound in the hellish <term>Tartarus</term> <note>A clever linguistic pun by the author: "Tartarus" refers to the deepest abyss of Hades in Greek mythology, sounding nearly identical to the chemical "Tartar" being discussed</note> of this age. The fortified castle of the Royal Palace is opened with the eternal Kingdom of Jesus Christ, and we shall become the true disciples of Christ, the "salt of the earth," which persists even in the heat of the fire because its innermost essence has been turned outward. For it is the Spirit of the eternal God alone that gives power and life.

> Also: "The salt of tartar <term>fixes</term> <note>Original: *sigiret* (from the Latin *fixare*), meaning to make a volatile substance stable so it does not evaporate when heated</note> powerfully. The spirit resides in the <term>Mercury</term>, seek the color in the <term>Sulphur</term>"—namely in the Tartar—"and the <term>coagulation</term> <note>The process of thickening or becoming a solid mass</note> in the salt; then you have three things which together can give birth to a perfect whole."

With these words, he indicates that the Stone consists of three things.

> Also: "The <term>Vegetable Stone</term> <note>Original Latin: *Lapis vegetabilis*. In alchemy, this refers to a transformation medium derived from the plant kingdom, particularly from the "blood" of the vine</note> is found in wine, as the noblest essence of all growing things,"

by which he only means the Tartar.

> "The spirit of wine has a wondrous property, for without it, no true <term>Tincture of the Sun</term> <note>Original: *Tinctur Solis*, a medicinal extract of gold used for healing and transformation</note> can be extracted, nor can a true <term>Potable Gold</term> <note>Original: *Aurum potabile*, a legendary alchemical medicine consisting of gold dissolved into a drinkable form</note> be made. But few know how to prepare the spirit of wine correctly, much less how to fathom its properties."

All of this refers to the Tartar.

# Saltpeter, Tartar, and Air are the Principles of the Stone.

The <term>Tartar</term> <note>Potassium bitartrate, the sediment from wine casks, viewed as a bridge between the plant and mineral worlds</note> is taken in place of gold, the <term>Tartar</term> and <term>Saltpeter</term> <note>Nitre; a salt-like mineral used as a powerful oxidizing agent in alchemy</note> in place of the grapevine, and the Air in place of the wine. In the mineral kingdom, gold is the noblest. In the vegetable kingdom, it is the wine, and in the animal kingdom, it is Man. All of this rests upon the deepest foundation of nature, and these analogies between the components of the Stone and such things descend from our father Adam; they were not merely invented by modern men.

---

->§. 16.<-

Likewise:
> "The <term>Salt of Tartar</term> is not recorded in the book of minerals, 
> "but is begotten from vegetable seed; yet it has received such 
> "power from its Creator that it possesses a miraculous love 
> "and affinity for the metals. There will not soon be born a 
> "wise master who will learn to explore all its secrets. 
> "However, making the <term>Stone of the Wise</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting long life</note> out of it 
> "cannot take place, for it is vegetable."

In this discourse, he has clearly identified <term>Tartar</term> as a component of the Stone—at least to the one who has understanding. To be sure, he had to conceal this again with his final words—claiming that the <term>Stone of the Wise</term> could not be made from it—because no possessor of the Stone was permitted to openly reveal its components. This was because a curse had been placed upon whoever disclosed the Stone. Thus, when they spoke of the materials of the Stone and named them, they had to do so with great caution, interweaving other talk, or pretending to speak of a different matter while mentioning the parts of the Stone by name.

However, when they spoke of the Stone itself, they never named its parts except through images and symbols. When he says that the Stone could not be made from <term>Tartar</term>, he is also correct; for when <term>Tartar</term> is taken alone, the Stone cannot be made from it. In the same way, the philosophers reject all <term>Salts</term> <note>original: "Salien" (from Latin *salia*)</note> and <term>Salt solutions</term>, even though they [contain] so

Behold, He comes, and His reward is with Him, and His retribution before Him! The beginning comes into the end, eternity comes into time! Make way, make way, and yield, all you gods and rulers of the earth, for the eternal **King of Glory** wishes to enter.'"

Concerning the Names of the Matters of the Stone.

# The Fourth Chapter.

## Of the Names of the Matters of the Stone, of the Fire and of the Water of the Sages.

->§. 1.<-

The preparation of the **Philosopher's Stone** requires, from the very beginning, a precisely measured proportion of the materials to one another, which must be observed absolutely. Otherwise, no proper <term>Agens</term> <note>original: *Agens*; the active or masculine principle in a chemical reaction</note> and <term>Patiens</term> <note>original: *Patiens*; the passive or feminine principle that receives the action</note>, nor any orderly <term>Regiment</term> <note>original: *Regiment*; the specific sequence of heat and governance of the alchemical process</note> can be expected. It cannot be otherwise, because the entire work must consist of a living, active essence of the **World Spirit** <note>original: *Weltgeist*; also known as the *Spiritus Mundi*, believed to be the universal life force animating all matter</note>, which must dissolve the salt-matter, and yet also be bound, <term>coagulated</term> <note>original: *coaguliret*; the process of thickening or solidifying a liquid</note>, and as it were, "killed" again by the salt. It follows naturally, then, that if one takes too much **Saltpeter**, the **Air** must be attracted too strongly and in too great a quantity; and if there is too much **Air**, it cannot naturally be bound by the **Tartar**, but the moist nature takes the upper hand and the salt becomes wet, whereby the work is then spoiled. There must, therefore, be a precisely measured proportion of the materials against one another; and because the Sages call these parts by certain names during the composition, I will first explain these names here.

### Three Principles. <note>original: *Drey Principia*; the fundamental building blocks of matter in Paracelsian alchemy: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note>

The **Stone** has three parts, which are first called the **Three Principles**. The **Saltpeter** is **Salt**, **Luna** <note>original: *Luna*; the Moon</note>, **Moon**, **Silver**: the **Tartar** is **Sulfur**, **Sol** <note>original: *Sol*; the Sun</note>, **Sun**, **Gold**, and is the fixed <note>stable or non-volatile</note> part of the **Stone**: the **Air** is **Mercury**, **Quicksilver** or <term>argentum vivum</term> <note>original: *argentum vivum*; Latin for "living silver" or liquid mercury</note>, and is the volatile <note>easily evaporated</note> part of the **Stone**. The **Saltpeter** is

### Two Dragons.

When the Stone is considered in two parts, as volatile and fixed, they are called **two dragons**, one of which has wings and the other none, and which fight with one another. <note>In alchemical symbolism, the winged dragon represents the volatile substance (that which evaporates), while the wingless dragon represents the fixed substance (that which remains stable in fire).</note>

### Steel and Magnet.

->§. 2.<-

This designation is especially used by *Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and chemist known for his work on the composition of air.</note> and *Philaletha* <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a pseudonym for the 17th-century alchemist George Starkey, famous for his "Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King."</note>. The **Steel** is the **Saltpeter**, which must prepare the **Tartar** <note>original: *Tartar*; a potassium salt crust found in wine barrels, used here as a primary material</note> so that it attracts the air and thus strikes the fire. The **Magnet** is therefore the **Tartar**, for a magnet is attractive, and it is the **Tartar** which attracts <note>original: *attrahiret*</note> the air.

*Philaletha* says:
> “Our Steel is the true key to the work, without which the lamp-fire cannot be lit by any art.”

The air creates the fire within the matter, which should never become too strong. However, without the Saltpeter, the air cannot enter the Tartar; therefore, it is the Steel that strikes the fire. Furthermore, he says:

> “It is the <term>ore</term> <note>original: *Minera*; the raw mineral or source from which a metal is extracted</note> of gold, the lower secret fire, an <term>epitome</term> <note>original: *Inbegriff*; a concentrated essence or summary</note> of the upper powers in the lower.”

The **Gold** is the **Tartar**, which lies beneath the Saltpeter like gold in its ore, for in this saltpetrous ore, the gold must grow into its power. It causes the attraction of the air in the Tartar; therefore, it is the lower secret fire and a concept of the upper powers in the lower.

> “The Magnet,”

he says,

> “is the <term>ore</term> of the Steel of the Sages.”

->§. 3.<-

The Tartar is also called the **Red Man** and the **King**; the Saltpeter with the air, however, is called the **White Woman** and the **Queen**. Likewise, the Tartar is the **Lion**, and the Saltpeter with the air is the **Eagle**, with which the Lion shall fight and overcome. Thus, Saltpeter has the same name as the air in many things, which is also quite natural, because it is most closely related to the air. Therefore, when fire is spoken of—which is actually the air—the Saltpeter is also meant as the fire. The fire should not be too strong, that is, the air should not reach the matter in too great a quantity. But if too much Saltpeter is taken in the first fusion, it happens precisely because too much air enters. Therefore, it is natural that Saltpeter must also be considered as the fire: and precisely for this reason *Philaletha* says it is the **lower secret fire**.

# Of the Names of the Substances of the Stone.

...water, or a watery fire. Likewise, the word <term>Schamajim</term> <note>original: *Schamajim*; the Hebrew word for the Heavens or Sky (*Shamayim*). Alchemists often interpreted this word as a combination of *esh* (fire) and *mayim* (water), symbolizing the union of opposites.</note>, the Heaven, which is of fire and water, and so the Heaven is called a fiery water, or a watery fire. Just as Saltpeter is understood to be within the fire, so Saltpeter is also understood to be within the water at the same time. Regarding both, we wish to hear the Philosophers.

## The Fire of the Sages.

->§. 4.<-

*Alanus* <note>Likely referring to Alanus ab Insulis (Alain de Lille), a 12th-century theologian and philosopher to whom various alchemical works were later attributed.</note>: “Our fire is twofold, dry and moist. The dry is elemental, and is in the body,” namely the Saltpeter, “but the moist fire is in the <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Merkur*; here referring to the "Philosophical Mercury," the volatile, life-giving principle, rather than common quicksilver.</note>,” in the air. Here one should note that the fire in the <term>subsequent work</term> <note>original: *Nacharbeit*; the later stages of the alchemical process, often involving the maturation or "ripening" of the Stone.</note> is often taken for the dry fire, and the fire in the <term>preliminary work</term> <note>original: *Vorarbeit*; the initial purification and preparation of the raw materials.</note> for the moist fire, because in the preliminary work the moisture has more power than in the subsequent work. Both they also call their dry and wet path in the work, where often one person rejects the dry path and another the wet, and yet both are right, depending on whether the preliminary or subsequent work is being considered. Through such rejection they have created a labyrinth for the foolish and unworthy, so that they should not sneak into this paradise-garden of wisdom. This was the sword of the Cherub moving to and fro before Paradise, which could be interpreted and turned in this way and also otherwise.

*Philaletha* <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist. His name means "peaceful lover of truth."</note>: “The fire with which we <term>corrupt</term> <note>original: *corrumpiren*; in alchemical theory, "corruption" is the breakdown of a substance's form, which must occur before it can be "generated" into a new, higher form.</note> and circulate is in the water. It is the fire of our water.” This is to be understood as being of both the Saltpeter and the air.

Likewise: “The water has an acting <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: *Schwefel*; in alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle of a substance.</note> in it.” This is the Saltpeter, within which the <term>Tartar</term> <note>original: *Tartar*; the sediment of wine, used here as a symbol for a specific salt or mineral "earth."</note> acts.

# Of the Names of the Substances of the Stone.

Likewise: Know, "that our fire is the internal fire of the <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: *Schwefel*; in alchemical theory, sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter.</note> of your water, which completes the whole work." This is the saltpeter, which is also the water, and in which the <term>tartar</term> <note>original: *Tartar* (or *Weinstein*); the potassium bitartrate crystals found in wine barrels, here used as a symbol for the earthy, fixed principle.</note> is also present as the sulfur. This fire of saltpeter carries out everything, and everything depends on this being taken in the right proportion from the start, for if too much of it is taken, then everything is ruined.

Likewise: "The preparation of this our fire occurs through <term>art</term> <note>original: *Kunst*; specifically the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy.</note>. All four elements are together in a single vessel, as fire, air, water, and earth, and without this there is no fire." Therefore, there is no coal-fire used with the Philosopher’s Stone; rather, the <term>spirit of the world</term> <note>original: *Geist der Welt*; the *Spiritus Mundi*, a vital, celestial essence believed to be the source of all life and movement.</note> brought into the matter is the fire, and within it are all four elements.

"This heavenly fire excites the elements and awakens them to action. This is the internal and sole <term>agent</term> <note>original: *Agens*; the active force that causes change in the material.</note> in our art. It is truly a very simple, and if one were to say it, a laughable secret, and yet the Sages open their <term>chaos</term> <note>original: *Chaos*; the primary, unorganized state of matter before it is refined into the Stone.</note> with this fire."

If one told someone: Take one part saltpeter and two parts tartar and melt them together; then every fool could do it, and thus there is no wisdom in the physical handling of the Stone, and the simplest person could make it. But in the causes—why things must be made thus, and which lie in the spirit and life of God and the world—to recognize these, therein lies the wisdom.

Likewise: "The acting principle," as the air, "is the heat, and the fire of the Sages. This is the moving instrument, and there is no other acting thing in the world. When this heat is gone, there is no movement. One must hide it in moist <term>dung</term> <note>original: *Mist*; a common alchemical metaphor for providing a gentle, natural, and steady heat, often achieved historically by burying vessels in fermenting horse manure.</note>, for our fire is contained in our spiritual dung-puddle." The matter is compared to dung, and the saltpeter in this matter must thus be prepared or proportioned...

# On the Names of the Materials of the Stone.

...always present, but the power of a well-regulated fire produces our Stone with less effort and cost, provided that one correctly sets the proportion with the <term>saltpeter</term> <note>original: *Salpeter*; potassium nitrate, which alchemists believed contained a concentrated form of the universal "spirit of the world."</note> at the beginning. For once our Stone has been dissolved into the first fire, it <term>calcinates</term>, <term>sublimes</term>, and <term>distills</term> <note>These are three of the core laboratory processes: reducing to powder by heat, turning a solid to vapor, and purifying a liquid through evaporation and condensation.</note> itself through the power of a well-proportioned fire, and perfects itself in a single vessel without any other manual labor. This applies to every **rotation**, in which the work perfects itself in its degree according to the arrangement. In the preliminary work, there are three rotations, each of ninety days; and in the subsequent work, there are seven, each of forty days, as we shall see in the following.

### §. 7.

**Pontanus**: <note>Johannes Pontanus (likely 15th century), an alchemist famous for his "Epistle" claiming there is only one "secret fire" necessary for the work.</note> "Our fire is mineral," which is the saltpeter, "even, ever-lasting, and does not smoke, unless it is too greatly awakened. It has sulfur," as the <term>tartar</term> <note>original: *Tartar*; the crusty deposit found in wine barrels, used here as a symbol for the "fixed" or earthy principle.</note>, "within it, and dissolves everything."

**Rosarius**: "I command all seekers of the Art to make a gentle fire in the beginning, until peace is established between fire and water. The fire must always be gentle until the water is <term>coagulated</term> <note>original: *coaguliret*; the process of thickening or becoming solid.</note>." Here, the air is taken as the water, and the fire is the saltpeter, of which there should not be too much; otherwise, the air cannot dry onto the salt or be coagulated, for too much saltpeter causes too much attraction of air. Everything depends on the beginning, for if it is not hit correctly there, everything is ruined, even if everything else were right.

Also: "The **philosophical fountain** is the beginning and foundation of the Art," namely the saltpeter under the tartar, for through it the air arrives. "It is the beginning and the end, the whole essence <note>original: *totum esse*; Latin for "total being" or "entirety of existence."</note> and

# On the Names of the Materials of the Stone.

"...and a 'Jack-of-all-trades' <note>original: *Factotum*; a Latin term meaning someone or something that does everything.</note>. The matter, or the Philosophical Subject <note>original: *Subjectum Sophicum*; the raw material used by the alchemists to begin their work.</note>, is found in the earth as a concentrated water and fire." That is: the saltpeter, which is water and fire, is within the tartar.

**Ripley** <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), an influential English alchemist and Augustinian canon, known for his "Twelve Gates" of alchemy.</note>: "Make a fire in your glass which burns the body more than the elemental fire," namely, than a coal fire.

### §. 8.

**Philalethes** <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes (1628–1665), a famous anonymous alchemist whose name means "peaceful lover of truth."</note>: "You must not think that the increase of the fire consists in the blowing of coals; no, truly, it is a subtle internal fire which we possess. It is the internal fire of the sulfur of your water which completes the whole work." Saltpeter is fire and water, and beneath it is the tartar acting as the sulfur. "The governance of the fire is a straightforward <term>decoction</term> <note>The process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a liquid to ripen it or extract its essence.</note>, from the beginning to the end of the work, and the entire mastery consists in vapor and steam," because the spirit is regarded as a subtle vapor.

**Likewise**: "With the secret sulfur that is in our water, which we divide quite precisely at the beginning, we open our body." The sulfur or tartar is in the water or saltpeter, and through this, the air is drawn in and the matter is opened. "For this fire can do that which no other fire can do."

**Likewise**: "You will not divide the elements if you have not wisely divided your materials at the beginning of the work. Well begun is half done." Everywhere, then, it is testified that everything depends upon the beginning.

**Likewise**: "Do not care so much for the fire of your <term>athanor</term> <note>A furnace used by alchemists designed to maintain a constant, steady heat for long periods without constant attention.</note> as for your internal fire. Seek the same in the house of the <term>Ram</term> <note>original: *Widder* (Aries). In alchemy, Aries is the sign of the spring, associated with the start of the work and the collection of the 'universal spirit' in saltpeter.</note>," in the saltpeter, "and draw it out from the depths of <term>Saturn</term> <note>Saturn is traditionally the planet/metal lead, but in this context, it refers to the heavy, 'dead' material or the hidden depths of the air.</note>," from the air. "Let <term>Mercury</term> <note>The 'messenger of the gods,' representing the volatile spirit or the liquid metal (quicksilver) that connects the fixed and volatile parts of the work.</note> be the negotiator, and your..."

# On the Names of the Materials of the Stone.

"...but the power of a well-governed fire creates our **Stone** with less effort and cost, specifically if one correctly determines the proportion with the saltpeter at the beginning. For once our **Stone** has been dissolved in the first fire, it **calcines**, **sublimes**, and **distills** itself through the power of a well-proportioned fire, and perfects itself in a single vessel without any other manual labor." This applies to every **rotation** <note>An alchemical cycle of transformation, often involving heating and cooling, intended to refine the substance.</note>, in which the work perfects itself in its own degree according to its arrangement. In the preliminary work, there are three rotations, each of ninety days; and in the subsequent work, there are seven, each of forty days, as we shall see in what follows.

->§. 7.<-

**Pontanus** <note>Johannes Pontanus, a 15th-century alchemist famous for his "Epistle" regarding the "secret fire" that he claimed to have discovered after many failures.</note>: "Our fire is mineral," which is the saltpeter, "equal, ever-lasting, and does not smoke unless it is too greatly awakened. It has the **sulfur**," referring to the tartar, "within itself, and dissolves everything."

**Rosarius** <note>Referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), one of the most famous illustrated alchemical treatises of the 16th century.</note>: "I command all seekers of the Art to make a gentle fire in the beginning, until peace has been made between fire and water. The fire must always be gentle until the water is **coagulated** <note>The process of a liquid thickening or becoming a solid; in alchemy, this often refers to the "spirit" becoming fixed in the "body."</note>." Here, the air is taken to be the water, and the fire is the saltpeter, of which there should not be too much; otherwise, the air cannot dry onto the salt or be coagulated, for too much saltpeter causes too much air to be drawn in. Everything depends upon the beginning, for if a mistake is made there, all is lost, even if everything else were correct.

Likewise: "The philosophical fountain <note>original: *Brünnlein*; a common alchemical metaphor for the source of the transformative solvent or "mercury."</note> is the beginning and ground of the Art," namely, the saltpeter beneath the tartar, for through this the air arrives. "It is the beginning and the end, the total essence" <note>original: *totum esse*; Latin for "the whole of being" or "the total essence."</note>

# On the Names of the Materials of the Stone.

> "Recognize the **Doves of Diana** <note>In alchemical symbolism, these represent the white, volatile spirits or vapors that rise during the purification of the material.</note>, the **tartar**, which represents the **Mercury** <note>The 'spirit' or volatile principle of a substance.</note> or the air and is itself made volatile by it; then these are the doves that bring the olive leaf in their mouths and announce that the waters have dried up." <note>A biblical allusion to Noah’s Ark, used here to signify the end of the 'moist' stage of the process.</note>

The **Aries** <note>The Ram, the first sign of the zodiac.</note> is the house of **Mars**, and Mars holds the fire; therefore, **saltpeter** is the Aries. Since saltpeter marks the beginning of the work and is the first substance one should take in hand, as we have seen above, they also say: “One should begin in March, when the sun enters Aries, and then one would find it in the **Vintage Month** <note>German: 'Weinmonat' (October), the time of the grape harvest when tartar—the solid sediment—is traditionally collected from wine casks.</note>,” that is, in the tartar.

Likewise: “Our **water** is our **vessel**, and within it dwells our **furnace** in a hidden manner, whose fire must be moderate so that the entire work is not destroyed. Our secret consists in this water; thus, the structure of the secret furnace is also founded upon the composition of the water. Within the knowledge of this lie all our fires, all weights, and all **regimens** <note>The 'regimen' refers to the governing of the fire's intensity and the timing of different stages in the alchemical process.</note> hidden. You must take care that you do not err at the very beginning.” All of this pertains to the correct proportion of the saltpeter—how much of it one should take at the start, and that one hits upon the right amount.

...understood and believed. Now, one bodily thing cannot help another; rather, that which is to help toward perfection must itself be perfect and spirit. It is also certain and true that in the birth and formation of the matter, spirit and body separate from each other, each into its own nature and condition, just like soul and body. And although there is still spirit and life in the bodily essence, it is already as if in a prison, and is to a certain extent bound in its strength and power to act. In such a condition, a means would first be necessary to set the spirit itself free from its bonds in the water, in order to be able to raise the matter of the Stone <term>Stone: the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of perfecting base metals and human health.</term> into the spiritual nature. But from where should one take such a thing, and how should one do it?

If it became even the slightest bit damp, it would have attracted too much; the moisture would have overcome the dryness, and the dryness could no longer act against it. There would no longer be any properties acting against each other, and no new birth could occur. This foundation is irrefutable and true, and therefore the Sages do not want any real or bodily water, as we shall now hear from their testimonies.

<meta>This page continues the technical argument that alchemical "dissolution" is a spiritual process rather than a literal one. <header>Concerning the Names of the Materials of the Stone.</header>

"We also dissolve into a water which does not make the hands wet. Note well, the more of your earth and the less of the water there is, the better your solution will be." Dryness must always have the upper hand; then the air is well dried onto the salt, and the salt is all the better dissolved. It is clear then from this that in the first composition one must take less saltpeter than tartar <term>tartar: potassium bitartrate, a salt found in wine barrels, used here as a physical base for the spirit.</term>.

Item: "Dissolve <note>original: *solvire*</note> what nature has previously bound, and transform your essential mercury <term>mercury: in alchemy, this refers to the "spirit" or volatile principle of a substance, not just the metal.</term> into wind and not into water. Without this, nothing will come of your work. Dryness must bring moisture to fixity <term>fixity: the state where a substance can withstand heat without evaporating.</term>, and the moisture," the air, "must make the earth volatile, so that from these things thus tempered a middle ground will arise."

*Concerning the Stone* <note>original: *De Lap.*, likely referring to the treatise *De Lapide Philosophorum*.</note>: "The first operation after the composition in our work is the solution <term>solution: the process of turning a solid substance into a liquid-like or spiritual state</term>, and the solution of the body happens simultaneously with the coagulation of the spirit, and the coagulation <term>coagulation: the process of making a spirit or vapor solid or "fixed."</term> of the spirit happens simultaneously with the solution of the body." All these speeches, however many there may be, can be applied to this subject and the working of the Stone; therefore, one can be beyond all doubt that this is the Stone and no other, and that the work is also correct. The spirit must be coagulated onto the salt as a spirit; therefore, it must not become water.

This is the Incarnation <term>Incarnation: the theological concept of spirit taking on flesh, used here to describe the union of the volatile World-Spirit with a solid physical base.</term> of Christ within us, when God as the Word becomes flesh in us. God also calls himself and his spirit a water of life, and says: I will pour water on the thirsty, and streams on the dry ground. I will pour my spirit on your offspring, and my blessing on your descendants. <note>A reference to Isaiah 44:3.</note> Just as this is not physical water in view of God’s eternal essence, but rather spirit which is merely called a water; so it is also in view of the external God or external nature, for the World-Spirit <term>World-Spirit: often called the *Anima Mundi*, this is the invisible vital force the alchemist seeks to "fix" into a physical form.</term> is also not physical water and must not become such a water, but must instead unite with the salt as a spirit. There is nothing higher or more powerful than God or spirit. Therefore, when the spirit of the world is united with the salt, it is like an Incarnation to which all power in heaven and on earth is given. Thus, the Philosopher’s Stone <note>original: *Lapis Philosophorum*</note> is the most perfect image of the spiritual stone, Jesus Christ, and for that reason, it possesses such great power. Whoever considers all of this cannot doubt that this stone described here in this book is the true Philosopher’s Stone <note>original: *Lapis Philosophorum*</note>, and he can also understand that the testimonies of the wise concerning their water and its drying onto the matter are truth.

### §. 13.

> Geber <note>Geber: the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a legendary figure in alchemy whose writings emphasized the "mercury-sulfur" theory of metals.</note>: “Those are mistaken who believe they can extract a clear, transparent water from Mercury,” that is, from the air, “and make many and wonderful things from it.”

The spirit, which is called a water, should remain in the form of spirit with the material of the salt, and should not thicken into water. It is the spirit alone which, as spirit, can unite with the material and yet remain spirit; however, physical water cannot become one with it and still remain water. No chemist will be able to apply these things to his false processes; but the wise have not spoken into the wind <note>original: "in Wind geredet"; a German idiom meaning to speak in vain or to no effect.</note>; here, however, they can all be applied, and for that reason, this is the true **Philosophers’ Stone** <term>Philosophers’ Stone: *Lapis Philosophorum*, the legendary goal of alchemy, here described as a spiritual-material union rather than a mere physical substance.</term>. ->§. 14.<-

The **Hermetic Philosopher** <note>A common title for an anonymous author in the Hermetic or alchemical tradition.</note> says: "When the Mercury begins to bloom from the gold," that is, when the material attracts the air during the process of crushing, "this is a sign of its blooming and its emergence, for there the gold has returned to its first root," namely, the material comes again into community and union with the air. "This Mercury is called the **manifest Stone of the Wise**," which is still outside the vessel, "over which great care and diligence must be taken so that, as soon as it arises, it is set for **fixation** <term>Fixation: The process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat so that it no longer evaporates.</term>, and held so that it does not fly away and perish. For as soon as it is prepared," when the salt is crushed and has attracted the air, "and one does not fix it immediately, it flies away." Therefore, one should fix it with its own body, "that is, with other fixed gold," which is **Tartar** <term>Tartar: Specifically "Crude Tartar" or potassium bitartrate, but here used as a philosophical name for a specific stage of the material.</term>.

The matter is this: When Tartar and Saltpeter <term>Saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, used here as an oxidizing agent in the melting process.</term> have been melted together and poured out, it must—as soon as it has become cold and hard—be crushed into a delicate flour. During this crushing, it attracts air, and one must hurry so that it does not receive too much air. To this crushed material, one then adds "plain Tartar," which is called the **Ferment**, and with this, one seals it in the vessel and lets it stand quietly. In the following parts of the process, one will see the rest.

"This is what **Medea** <note>original: "Meda"; a reference to the Greek sorceress Medea, who in myth rejuvenated the elderly Aeson by cutting him into pieces and boiling him in a cauldron with magical herbs.</note> teaches concerning the old Sage who wished to become young again, in that he should allow himself to be divided and boiled until his perfect decoction was reached, and no further. "These are the two elements that one joins together, as moist and dry. The **Mercury** that is extracted is moist, liquid, and volatile; but the dry is the body and the **ferment**, with which we retain and fix the **Mercury**. This body is called the **hidden Stone**. The body has the power hidden within itself, which is extracted from it through the **Mercury**. In such a way, the right joining of the elements occurs—the active and the passive—and thus nature itself performs everything, and the artist only prepares the disposition of the material and assists nature. ### §. 15.

This description of the ferment is crafted with prudence and understanding. It hints sufficiently at what the ferment may be, and how one must use it immediately during the first preparation; yet it is also so well hidden that no <term>Sophist</term> <note>In alchemical literature, a "sophist" refers to a false practitioner or charlatan who follows the literal recipes without understanding the underlying spiritual or natural principles.</note> has been able to see what the <term>Ferment of the Sages</term> might be. This part, above all, had to be kept hidden, and it served as a special bolt across this <term>Paradise Garden</term> <note>A metaphor for the successful completion of the alchemical "Great Work" or the state of primordial perfection.</note>; so much so, that even if one discovered the right <term>Matter</term> and the way to process it, but did not know this ferment, all was in vain. Therefore, in all things, it is with this <term>Stone</term> as it is with Christ: among all those who have found it, it must be tested in the fire. Even when someone attained the true knowledge of the matter, the <note>original: "die Ochsen am Berge gestanden"</note> oxen stood baffled before the mountain <note>A German idiom meaning to be completely stuck or unable to see the way forward despite being right in front of the goal.</note> as to what should be done with it. Many have spent four to ten years on all sorts of experiments before they reached the goal and found one thing after another. He who was firm and well-grounded in the truth of the knowledge—that it was impossible for there to be any other matter for the <term>Stone of the Sages</term> than this—he endured and stayed with it, no matter how often he failed, and did not allow himself to be led astray, neither by his failed attempts nor by other plausible <term>sophistries</term>. But he who was not firm within himself in the knowledge of things, he threw the matter away again when he failed once and it did not immediately bring him gold. He threw away the life of the world and chose for himself death in <term>minerals</term> or other dead things.

It is exactly the same with Jesus Christ in His revelation. This revelation of Jesus Christ has come into the hands of various people...

### Concerning the Names of the Materials of the Stone.

...taken together is also the water; and this water is united with the mature and fixed sulfur as with the <term>tartar ferment</term> <note>A catalytic substance derived from potassium bitartrate used to "leaven" or transform the alchemical body</note>, and this is then repeated.

->§. 18.<-

This method of calling different things by the same name stems from the wisdom of God, and the same occurs in God’s Word. The Spirit of God, or of Jesus Christ, is distinct from the corrupt human, just as the <term>World-Spirit</term> <note>Latin: *spiritus mundi*; a vital, universal essence that alchemists sought to capture from the air or dew to animate their work</note> in itself is distinct from the <term>Salt</term> <note>In alchemy, Salt typically represents the physical body, solidity, and the principle of fixity</note>. But when the Spirit of Christ enters into the human, becomes human within them and enlivens them, then the believers are also called "Christ," just as the Salt is also called "Water" when it has the Spirit of the World within itself. Behold, therefore, what a being the <term>Stone of the Sages</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy, believed to be a perfect balance of spirit and matter</note> is! It contains the spirit and the life of the outer God of Nature within itself, just as the believers have the Spirit of the eternal God within them. And as the spirit in the Stone works upon its salt-body and places it into its own nature, so that the body becomes like pure spirit; exactly so shall the eternal Spirit of God work in the future in all humans and in all of nature on earth, so that we shall be in soul and body as pure spirit. There, another heart, mind, and disposition shall be. There, the <term>Water of Life</term> marries the <term>Gold</term> or <term>King</term>—that is, the outer nature, which will rule in divine power. There, humans are made into kings and priests through this spirit. There shall be the <term>Wedding of the Lamb</term> <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Revelation, here used as an allegory for the final, perfect union of spiritual essence and physical matter</note>, where the spirit will be wedded to matter. There, the Spirit of God will remain with matter, and there will be a tabernacle of God with men on earth, and God will dwell within them. There, the Spirit of God is the permanent Water of Eternal Life that remains with matter; therefore, no more sickness, and no more death or dying is found there. Of this, the Stone is the essential image, in that it [heals] all sicknesses...

It frames the creation of the Philosopher's Stone as a physical mirror of the biblical "Wedding of the Lamb," symbolizing the eternal union of spirit and matter.</summary>

...[infirmi]ties and heals; therefore it is the fruit from the Paradise of God, for only in Paradise is there no sickness or death. Such things the <term>Stone of the Sages</term> <note>Also known as the Philosopher's Stone; here used as a metaphor for spiritual perfection rather than just a tool for making gold</note> teaches us, and it points us toward that, rather than toward gold and silver. A person can possess the Stone of the Sages and yet be outside the life of the holy God. What would the Stone of the Sages, and all the gold and silver made with it, help a person now if he himself were not <term>tinctured</term> <note>To be "tinctured" is to be thoroughly permeated and transformed by a spiritual essence, much like a dye changes the color of a cloth</note> with the Spirit of the eternal God? Would it not be a folly if he had tinctured so many dead metals and had forgotten his own self? The Stone comes from the Paradise of God and points humanity toward Paradise. Therefore, with the Stone, we should direct our eyes toward the now-coming Paradise of God, where the whole of nature on earth will be a blessed stone of the wisdom of God, and we should pray to God that He might allow us to take part in it and not exclude us. This should be our endeavor, and not to look back like <note>original: "Loths Weib"</note> Lot’s wife <note>A biblical reference to Genesis 19:26; looking back at the material world leads to spiritual stagnation</note> toward gold and silver. From its revelation and its being made public, however, the scoffers of the announcement of the Last Judgment should see that God does not lack the means to put an end to the current reign of injustice; for this Stone too must strike and crush the entire image of regimes and constitutions on earth.

# On the Names of the Substances of the Stone.

...[by their ow]n body they have been blinded, and have wanted to take gold as the ferment.

Item: 
> "The Art adds to the raw, cold, and moist <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, volatile, and transformative principle, not just the liquid metal</note> the means to dissolve the mature gold, and from both, through mixing and secret composition, makes a Mercury that is drawn out of the **Red Servant** <note>original: "rothen Knecht"; a common alchemical code name for the solar principle or purified sulfur</note>, which is called the **Water of Life**, and this water does not wet the hands. Let your diligence be directed solely toward bringing this Mercury into being."

First, the <term>Tartar</term> <note>Often referring to the salt or "crust" found in the vessel; here it represents a specific stage of the matter</note> found in the molten mass joins the Mercury, acting as the air; then the Tartar-Ferment comes to this ensouled mass as a gold which is dissolved within it. When the **rotation** <note>A cyclical process of heating, evaporation, and condensation lasting a set number of days</note> of ninety days has ended, the entire mass is also a Mercury, to which its Tartar-Ferment or gold is added once again; and so each subsequent stage always becomes a double Mercury compared to the previous one.

Item, <term>Philaletha</term> <note>Referring to Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist and author of "Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King"</note>: 
> "**Calcination** <note>The process of reducing a substance to a powder through heat, often the first step in the Great Work</note> is to be done through the Mercury," namely through the matter ensouled with air, as the threefold water. "When this is added to the gold in proper proportion," as to the Tartar-Ferment; "it softens it, makes it raw, and through its internal heat awakens the heat of the gold, which then works into the moisture and dries it out. This is the true key of the work: namely, to make the ripe raw again by adding the unripe."

These words refer solely to the ferment which one is supposed to add, as can be clearly seen. The proportion of the substances must remain the same. Therefore, if a new ferment is to be added at the end of a rotation, it naturally follows that as much of the mass—the Mercury—must be removed as the amount of ferment that is to be added to it. That is why he says that one must add the Mercury to the gold in the proper proportion.

->§. 22.<-

Elsewhere *Philaletha* says: "The Stone is made from one thing, and from four mercurial substances."
If one takes the threefold water, and then the *Ferment* <note>The substance—usually gold or silver—added to the prepared matter to "leaven" it and finalize its transformation.</note>, which must indeed also become a *Mercury* and one with them, then there are four, and these are then a single thing. The three repetitions of the preliminary work and the final work, taken as one, also make four.
The threefold water, which is also called a *Mercury*, is also called a *Hermaphrodite* <note>A symbolic being combining male and female; in alchemy, it represents a substance that is both fixed and volatile, or "acid" and "alkaline."</note>, being of the male and female sex. The *Tartar* is the man, the *Saltpeter* is the woman, and from both comes the *Mercury*; therefore it is so named. A subsequent *Rotation* <note>The "Rotation of the Elements" or repeated cycles of distillation and fixation.</note> is also more perfect than the preceding one, and in the final work, the *Mass* is more perfect than in the preliminary work. # On the Names of the Substances of the Stone. 77

...names of the Stone, they speak now of the preliminary work, and now of the subsequent work <term>subsequent work: the final stages of the Great Work, often called the "rework" or "after-work"</term>, which confuses the ignorant because they are not used to paying attention to the parables and comparisons of one thing against another. God's Word, however, is exactly the same; it often expresses quite contrary things under the same name, as is evident from the names Jerusalem, Vineyard, Grapevine—indeed, from all the names in the Word of God, and even from the names of God Himself. One and the same thing is not always meant by a single name; for the wine in the cup of Babylon and the wine as the blood of Christ are things that are diametrically opposed to each other. But the essence of the matter, and the contrast of one against the other, soon provide understanding if one only pays attention.

Even though the sages have often thrown everything together and confused one with the other, the essence of the matter and the natural possibility of things allow one to recognize what they are talking about—whether the above or the below, the volatile or the fixed, the moist or the dry, the preliminary or the subsequent work—provided there is good reflection and consideration. Those who were true sages, such as **Philaletha** <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, an influential 17th-century alchemist</note>, **Ripley** <note>George Ripley, 15th-century English alchemist</note>, **Basilius** <note>Basilius Valentinus, the legendary monk-alchemist</note>, **Dornaeus** <note>Gerard Dorn, a follower and editor of Paracelsus</note>, **Paracelsus**, and many others, have fairly maintained the rules of wisdom in their writings regarding the chemical riddles, which in themselves date back to the beginning of the world. However, a great multitude of chatterers are not worth reading: there is not a spark of wisdom in them, and the images they have devised fit together like a "fist on the eye" <note>A German idiom meaning things are completely mismatched or jarringly inappropriate</note>. Meanwhile, these people imagined their work must pass for wisdom because they knew how to make the Stone. How and in what way some attained the Stone, I will leave undecided; but this much I say with certainty: that there have been truly godless, unscrupulous villains among them, who filled their books with godless, irresponsible things to lead others astray, and often wrote that one

# Chapter Five.

## On the First Preparation and Proportion of the Parts of the Substances of the Stone.

->§. 1.<-

Because the parts belonging to the **Stone** <term>Stone: The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical "Great Work"</term> are distinguished into dry and moist, fixed and volatile, it necessarily follows that these parts must also have their certain measured proportions; otherwise, an effect orderly according to the laws of nature could not possibly occur.

The proportion of the parts of the Stone is twofold: internal and external. The internal proportion is used in the first fusion of **saltpeter** and **tartar** <note>Saltpeter (potassium nitrate) and Tartar (potassium bitartrate) were common starting materials in alchemical recipes, often reacted together to create a "secret fire" or alkaline solvent</note>, and is as 1 to 2—namely, one part saltpeter and two parts tartar. For because the saltpeter creates the fire and is of a moist nature, together with the **Mercury** <note>In this context, Mercury refers to the philosophical principle of volatility and spirit rather than common quicksilver</note> as the air, but the dryness must remain master, and...

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

...is intended to bind the **Mercury** <term>Mercury: In alchemy, the "spirit" or volatile principle of a substance, not always referring to the liquid metal</term>; thus it follows naturally that there must be more **Tartar** <note>Tartar: A salt found in wine barrels; in alchemy, it represents a "magnetic" or grounding substance</note> than **Saltpeter** <note>Saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, used here as a "fiery" reactive agent</note>, for the fire must not become too strong.

The external proportion is used for the **ferment** <term>Ferment: A refined substance added to the work to initiate a specific chemical or spiritual transformation</term>, and is also one part to two parts, but reversed—namely, one part Tartar to the ferment, and two parts of the matter animated with air. This is again quite natural because the matter animated with air is to be regarded as the spirit or Mercury in relation to the ferment, and the spirit is meant to dissolve the body (as the ferment), make it perfect, and transmute it into its own nature; therefore, there must be more of the spirit than the body, as the philosophers also say. In regard to the internal proportion, there must be less spirit, and in regard to the external proportion, there must be more spirit. This discrepancy is the furthest and final labyrinth in which a seeker must wander until he can find his way out, and many a person has had to wander therein for several years.

The Sages have most hidden the internal proportion, while more thought is given to the external one, because the latter is of no use so long as one does not yet possess the former. Let us hear the philosophers on both.

->§. 2.<-

This is the first fusion, just as he explicitly calls it the first; the 8 ounces signify nothing, for one can start with much or little, and he only intends the first preparation...

No one has ever revealed more than this Philaletha <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes was a famous 17th-century alchemist, likely the American George Starkey, whose identity remained a mystery for centuries</note>, but he has also concealed it again with such skill that no one has been able to find the secret so easily; in this case, it was permitted for them to mix in all sorts of other things to keep the unworthy away.

The **fiery dragon** is the **saltpeter** <note>Potassium nitrate, used as an oxidizing agent</note>, because it creates the fire, as was proven in the previous chapter. The **magnetic body**, however, is the **tartar** <note>Potassium bitartrate, a salt found in wine casks</note>, because it is placed in a state of great hunger by the saltpeter to attract the air. The melted mass is then called the **Arsenicum** <term>Arsenic: In this context, it refers to a volatile, poisonous "first matter" or mineral vapor, not necessarily the pure element arsenic</term>, which, when considered by itself and without the mercury or the air, is compared to poison, which is meant to be transformed through the mercury during the work.

### §. 3.

> Philaletha: "Take four parts of our **fiery dragon**, who has the **Steel of the Sages** hidden in his belly, and nine parts of our **magnet**; mix them together through our **strong Vulcan** <note>Vulcan is the Roman god of fire; here it refers to a high-heat furnace</note> in the form of a mineral water, upon which a foam will swim—take that away. Throw away the shell, and choose the nut. Purify it for the third time through fire and salt, which will be easy to do when **Saturn** <term>Saturn: The alchemical symbol for lead, or the "heavy," dark stage of the process</term> beholds his form in the **Mirror of Mars** <term>Mars: The alchemical symbol for iron</term>."

This is likewise the internal proportion or the first preparation over a charcoal fire. For the fiery dragon or saltpeter, he takes four parts, and for the magnet or tartar, nine parts, which is slightly more tartar than a ratio of two parts to one part saltpeter. This is all the safer, but the more tartar there is, the stronger the charcoal fire must be; otherwise, it cannot be brought into flux, which is why he also adds a "strong Vulcan" there. The saltpeter creates the flux, which is why he calls it a "mineral water." It also seethes violently together and produces an impure

# On the Proportion of the Materials.
L
<meta>catchword: takes</meta>

...foam, which however must consume itself, and it must become completely clear; therefore he says: one should remove the foam and choose the nut <note>In alchemical allegory, the "nut" or "kernel" represents the essential, purified matter left after the "shell" or "chaff" of impurities has been discarded</note>.

All of this is indicated as faithfully as anything can be, yet here he leaves out the external proportion regarding the **ferment** <term>Ferment: In alchemy, a substance added to a base material to "leaven" it and trigger its transformation into a higher state, similar to how yeast transforms dough</term>. Even if one prepared it this way and proceeded, it would all be in vain, for the ferment must perform the main task; he describes this sufficiently in other places, but in such a way that no one would recognize it as the ferment. The "three-fold purification" refers to the three repetitions in the preliminary work, which he only hints at here, though he says more about it elsewhere.

What Philaletha <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, the pseudonym for a renowned 17th-century alchemist</note> says here about the mineral water—that one should discard the foam and take the nut—Paracelsus <note>The famous 16th-century Swiss-German physician who revolutionized alchemy and medicine</note> says in other words: namely, that one should only take the **blood of the Red Lion** and the **blossoms of the White Eagle**, for the **Tartar** is the **Red Lion** and the **Saltpeter** is the **White Eagle**.

->§. 4.<-

**Basilius.** <note>Basilius Valentinus (Basil Valentine), a legendary 15th-century alchemist-monk known for his writings on antimony and the "Twelve Keys"</note> This godly, wise man says: "A woman is half a body, and the man likewise," by which he points to two materials: **saltpeter** and **tartar**. "Too much moisture and too much dryness is harmful. Therefore, to conquer the desired goal, a very specific measure or proportion must be observed in the composition of the philosophical liquid essence, so that the greater is not too powerful for the smaller, nor the smaller too weak for the greater."

In the work of the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> itself, the **Greater** is the matter animated with air, while the **Smaller** is the **tartar-ferment**. If too much saltpeter is taken in the first fusion, then later in the work, the Greater becomes too powerful against the Smaller—that is, against the tartar-ferment—for the moisture...

<summary>This page decodes the allegorical language of Philaletha and Paracelsus, identifying the "Red Lion" as tartar and the "White Eagle" as saltpeter. It emphasizes the necessity of a specific balance between these substances, citing Basil Valentine's teachings on the proportions of the "philosophical liquid essence."</summary>

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

...takes the upper hand, and the **Tartar** <term>Tartar: A crystalline salt (potassium bitartrate) found in wine casks, often used in alchemy as a purifying agent or salt</term> cannot bind the **Mercury** <term>Mercury: In this context, the "philosophical" mercury or the volatile spirit that needs to be "fixed" or stabilized</term>. In order to maintain the correct measure, he says:

> “A two-fold fiery man must be fed with a white swan. 
> “These must be killed together, and at the same time 
> “made alive again.”

The fiery man is **Tartar**, which should be two-fold or two parts. The other, the white swan, is **Saltpeter** <term>Saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, associated with "air" and "fire" due to its role in combustion; here it acts as the "swan" because of its white color</term>. These two are to be killed together, which occurs during the melting together over a strong fire. Afterward, they draw in the air, and are made alive again through the air during the processing of the **Stone** <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life</note>.

This is the first preparation over the coal fire; here, he says nothing about the **ferment** <term>Ferment: A substance added to a base material to trigger its transformation into a higher state</term> and the external proportion, but speaks only of the successful progress of the work once this beginning has been made correctly. The external proportion follows immediately after, but first we wish to see the continuation of his words here.

He says:
> “The air from the four corners of the world must 
> “occupy three parts of the fiery man's sealed dwelling, 
> “so that the swan-song may be heard, to sing its farewell.” <note>original: "Valete" (Latin for "farewell") and "musiciren" (to perform music)</note>

When the matter is melted and has drawn in air, it is the "fiery man," and it must be enclosed in a vessel, which is then the dwelling of the fiery man. This glass must be so large relative to the enclosed matter that three parts are empty and one part is filled with the matter. These three parts fill with air, which acts upon the matter in the glass during the time of a **rotation** <term>Rotation: A cyclical alchemical process, often involving repeated heating, cooling, or circulation of substances</term>, dissolving <note>original: "solviren"</note> or opening it up, and the air must dry itself into it; thus, the air occupies three parts of the interior dwelling. With these words, he has also indicated the proportion of the vessel relative to the matter, which we will see further later on.

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

When things are thus arranged, then the **swan song** <note>An alchemical metaphor for the final, harmonious stage of a chemical reaction where a volatile substance is "fixed" or stabilized</note> can be heard in its ballet; that is, the **saltpeter** and **mercury** must submit, and the **tartar** <term>Tartar: A salt (potassium bitartrate) derived from wine casks, valued in alchemy for its purifying and stabilizing properties</term> maintains the upper hand through its dryness. “Then the roasted swan shall be a food for the king,” namely the **tartar**, “and the fiery king will greatly love the lovely voice of the queen,” namely the air and **saltpeter**, “and will take her to himself because of great love, and satisfy himself with her, until they both disappear and go into a single body.”

This was first a discourse on the effect of the materials in the vessel, and now he describes the external proportion, when he speaks: “It is said that two could suppress and overcome the third, especially if they have room to exercise their malice,” that is, when the glass vessel has its proper empty space <note>Alchemists were very careful about the "ullage" or air space in a vessel to prevent explosions or allow for proper circulation of vapors</note>.

“Thereupon you should know, for right reason, that a double wind must come, called **Volturnus** <note>original: "Volturnus", the Latin name for the Southeast wind; here it signifies two parts of a specific volatile vapor</note>,” namely two parts of the animated matter, “and thereafter a single wind,” as one part of **tartar ferment**. “These will roar and move greatly from the **East** and **South** <note>original: "Orient und Mittag"; in alchemical tradition, directions correspond to specific stages of heating or qualities of the material</note>. And when they have both been numbed, and their movement has passed, and water has come from air, then you may boldly trust that from a spiritual thing, a bodily thing will become, and that the number shall rule through the four seasons of the year in the **fourth heaven**, and complete its course in the lowest dwelling of the **palace**, and pass its highest examination after the **seven planets** <note>The seven planets of classical astronomy correspond to the seven metals: Gold (Sun), Silver (Moon), Mercury (Mercury), Copper (Venus), Iron (Mars), Tin (Jupiter), and Lead (Saturn)</note> have completed their reign; and so then the two that went out have suppressed and consumed the third.”

The year of the whole **Stone** <term>Stone: The "Philosopher's Stone," the ultimate goal of alchemy, capable of transmuting metals and perfecting the human soul</term> from beginning to end, has three repetitions in the preliminary work, which are three seasons in which fermentation occurs; and the entire subsequent work, in which there is no further addition with fermentation, is the fourth season and the fourth heaven.

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

The first rotation in the preliminary work is winter, which is cold and moist; the second is spring, when the fire of nature begins to grow; the third is summer, when the heat and drying are complete; and the post-work <note>The "post-work" or *Nacharbeit* refers to the final stages of refinement after the main chemical reactions have stabilized.</note> is autumn, when harvesting occurs throughout the seven repetitions, and the seven planets complete their reign.

In this way, Basilius <note>Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note> has all three measures—the internal and external, and the measure of the vessel relative to the material—together in this single figure. These two, Philaletha <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a 17th-century alchemist, author of "Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King"</note> and Basilius, are the only ones known to me who have revealed the inner proportion alongside the outer; the others have all given the outer proportion, beneath which the inner naturally lies in reverse, for it is always one to two. Thus Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus, a 13th-century philosopher and theologian associated with alchemy</note> says: To make the philosophical egg, the best proportion is a <term>Sesquialtera</term> <note>original: "Sesquialtera", a Latin term for a ratio of 1.5 to 1 (3:2). Here, the author links it to a 1:2 relationship.</note>, that is, one and a half, which is one to two; but one would have to sweat until he could derive one from the other.

### The Outer Proportion.

->§. 5.<-

> Philaletha: “Take one part of the Sun,” as tartar, “and two parts of the Moon,” as the matter animated by air, “which makes three parts of the body, to which add four parts of Mercury, which is one part more of the spiritual than the physical.”

The matter animated with air, as the triple water, is also taken as three Mercuries, and the air in the empty space of the glass is the fourth. These must come to the <term>Tartar-Ferment</term> <note>A substance derived from tartar used as a "yeast" to trigger transformation in the alchemical mixture.</note> and become one with it.

The body consists of three parts: the ferment and the two parts of the animated matter, which also the spiritual

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

The spiritual part is to be considered in relation to what is called the ferment; thus, one part of the spiritual is more than the physical, which in this sense is taken as the ferment. However, Mercury <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of volatility and fluidity, not just the liquid metal.</note> testifies that this division must concern the external proportion of the ferment relative to the animated matter; for in the first or internal proportion, there is as yet no Mercury, which is to be noted in all following testimonies regarding proportion.

Likewise, *Philaletha* <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist.</note>: “To prepare the water. Let the

“soul of each, leaving behind the coarse matter, 
“unites with the other and flees to the peaks of the mountain: 
“but do not let them remain there so long until they coagulate, 
“for that would spoil your work.” 
This refers to the stirring of the molten matter, where one should not delay so long that it draws in too much air, which would then coagulate into coarse moisture. This coarse matter of the spirit should remain behind, and only the soul of each should unite with the other, and one should seal it with the ferment, which he now describes as follows:

Therefore, he also says in this very place: “At 
“the least, it shall be guided three to seven times 
“through water and fire.” 
These are the three repetitions in the preliminary work and seven in the post-work, which also stand against each other as water

# On the Proportion of Materials.

The first rotation in the preliminary work is winter, which is cold and moist; the second is spring, when the fire of nature begins to grow; the third is summer, when the heat and dryness reach their peak; and the subsequent work is autumn, when the harvest is always gathered in the seven repetitions, and the seven planets complete their reign <note>This refers to the cyclical heating and cooling of the matter, mapping the laboratory process onto the four seasons and the astrological cycles.</note>.

In this way, Basilius <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note> has all three measures—the internal, the external, and the measure of the vessel against the matter—together in this single figure. These two, Philaletha <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes</note> and Basilius, are the only ones known to me who have revealed the internal proportion along with the external; the others have all given the external proportion, beneath which the internal certainly lies in an inverted manner, for it is always one to two. Therefore, Albertus <note>Albertus Magnus, the 13th-century philosopher and theologian</note> says: To make the philosophical egg, the best proportion is a <term>Sesquialtera</term> <note>original: "Sesquialtera"; traditionally a ratio of 3:2 or 1.5 to 1.</note>, which is one and a half, which is one to two <note>The author's mathematical explanation "one to two" for *sesquialtera* is unusual; in alchemy, these ratios often refer to the weight of the "mercury" relative to the "sulfur" or ferment.</note>; but one would have to sweat until he could take one out of the other.

> „most subtle scent,“ the air, „circulate so long until the 
> „soul of each, leaving behind the coarse matter, 
> „unites with the other and flees to the peaks of the mountain:

> „for that would spoil your work.“

These are the three repetitions in the preliminary work and seven in the subsequent work, which also [stand] against each other as water...

### Proportion of the Vessel to the Matter.

->§. 6.<-

Because, regarding the dissolution <note>original: "Solution"; the process of turning a solid into a liquid state.</note> of the body by the spirit, that which dissolves must be stronger than that which is to be dissolved; it is therefore according to nature that the air in the empty part of the glass must occupy a larger space than the matter which is to be dissolved. For this reason, there must also be a proportion of the vessel to the matter, and according to the testimony of the philosophers, it is as follows:
