# On the Proportion of the Materials.

Father-Heart: „Take great care that, in respect to your matter, three parts of the vessel remain empty, and only the fourth part be filled with matter.“

Hermogenes <note>A name often attributed to various alchemical and Hermetic writings.</note>: „Take the <term>Azoth</term> <note>original: Azoth; the "universal solvent" or the essential mercurial spirit sought by alchemists.</note>, put it into a vessel of which only the fourth part is filled.“

Likewise: „The <term>weight</term> <note>original: Pondus</note> is the point of all points, and is the most noble and important truth of the Art, in which all the Sages agree. Therefore, take the <term>Azoth</term>, the golden water, the <term>mercury of the sages</term>, and put it into a vial, so that only the fourth part of the glass is filled.“

These names—<term>Azoth</term>, golden water, and <term>mercury of the sages</term>—can well be given to the entire mass, for in the subsequent work it is truly such. Now, however, the space in the glass must be the same in both the preliminary work and the subsequent work. They prefer to use the description of the matter from the subsequent work <note>Alchemists often used the names of the final, perfected substances to describe the starting materials as a way to "veil" or hide their secrets from the uninitiated.</note> to indicate the size of the empty space in the glass, which also serves simultaneously to conceal the meaning.

Nature’s Proof: „Take the <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum vivum</note>, which is mixed with its active <term>sulfur</term>,“ namely the matter animated with air, which is mixed with the <term>ferment</term>. „Put it all into a single vessel, into a furnace that is one-third <term>buried</term> <note>original: inhumiret; likely referring to the practice of placing the vessel in a "bath" of sand or ash within the furnace to distribute heat evenly.</note>.“ It is actually only the <term>Tartar</term>, as the third part, which is "buried" by the other two parts; however, the number three can also be applied to the empty space in the glass.

Senior <note>Referring to "Senior Zadith" (Ibn Umail), a 10th-century Arabic alchemist whose works were influential in the Latin West.</note>: „Take one part of the ore, and three parts of the water. Or amalgamate three parts of mercury with one part of gold.“
Here, the total matter is both the "ore" and the "gold." The empty space in the glass, filled with air, is the "mercury" and also the "water," of which there should be three parts.

Cadmon in the *Turba* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers) is one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a meeting of ancient philosophers.</note>: „From the right body take one part,“ namely of the total matter: „and three parts of the air,“

> **Vade Mecum** <note>Latin for "Go with me"; a common title for a practical handbook or guide.</note>: “Take one part of the perfect red body,” Tartar Ferment, “and two parts of our <term>arsenic</term> <note>original: Arsenici; here likely used as a symbolic name for a volatile substance rather than the modern poisonous element.</note>, which takes the place of the woman <note>Alchemists used gendered metaphors to describe chemical reactions, often depicting the union of a "male" (fixed) and "female" (volatile) substance.</note>,” as the melted and air-animated matter. “Make everything subtle, put it into the philosophical vessel, and boil it well. For through this arrangement, the body is <term>dissolved</term> <note>original: solviret</note>, and both die, and also come to life again.”

## Proportion of the Vessel to the Matter.

->§. 6.<-

Because in regard to the <term>dissolution</term> <note>original: Solution</note> of the body by the spirit, that which dissolves must be stronger than that which is to be dissolved; it is therefore according to nature that the air in the empty part of the glass must occupy a larger space than the matter which is to be dissolved. Therefore, there must also be a proportion of the vessel to the matter, and according to the testimony of the philosophers, it is as follows:

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

**Fatherly Heart** <note>Likely referring to a specific alchemical text or a paternal persona used in a dialogue-style treatise.</note>:
> “Take good care that, regarding your material, three parts of the vessel remain empty, and only the fourth part is filled with material.”

**Hermogenes** <note>A legendary figure often associated with Hermes Trismegistus in alchemical literature.</note>:
> “Take the <term>Azoth</term> <note>The universal solvent or the "Mercury of the Sages," believed to be the essential agent of transformation.</note>, put it into a vessel, of which only the fourth part is filled.”

Also:
> “The <term>Weight</term> <note>original: Pondus; in this context, it refers to the volumetric proportion or 'weight' of the air compared to the substance.</note> is the <term>point of points</term> <note>original: Punctum punctorum; the most essential secret or core principle.</note>, and is the most noble and important truth of the Art, in which all the Sages agree. Therefore, take the Azoth, the golden water, the Mercury of the Sages, and put it into a vial, such that only the fourth part of the glass is filled.”

These names—Azoth, golden water, and Mercury of the Sages—can well be given to the whole <term>mass</term> <note>original: Massa; the combined mixture of all ingredients.</note>, for in the <term>subsequent work</term> <note>original: Nacharbeit; the later stages of the alchemical process.</note> it is truly such. Now, however, the space in the glass must be the same in both the <term>preliminary work</term> <note>original: Vorarbeit; the initial preparation of the materials.</note> and the subsequent work. They prefer to use the description of the material from the subsequent work to indicate the size of the empty space in the glass, which simultaneously serves as a way to conceal the secret.

**Proof of Nature:**
> “Take the <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum vivum</note> that is mixed with its acting <term>sulfur</term> <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle, while Mercury represents the passive, volatile, and feminine principle.</note>,”

namely the material animated with air, which is mixed with the <term>ferment</term> <note>A substance, often gold or silver, added to the "Stone" to stabilize it and give it the power to transmute metals.</note>.

> “Put it all into a single vessel, into a furnace that is <term>buried</term> <note>original: inhumiret; the practice of placing the vessel in a warm medium like sand, ash, or dung (a "philosopher's mine") to maintain steady heat.</note> to the third part.”

The <term>Tartar</term> <note>A salt-like deposit; here used as a specific chemical component of the work.</note>, as the third part, is actually the only one which is "buried" by the other two parts, but the number three can also be applied to the empty space in the glass.

**Senior** <note>Refers to Senior Zadith (Ibn Umail), a 10th-century Arabic alchemist whose works were highly influential in Europe.</note>:
> “Take one part of the ore and three parts of the water. Or amalgamate three parts of Mercury with one part of gold.”

Here, the entire material is the ore and also the gold. The empty space in the glass, filled with air, however, is the Mercury and also the water, of which there should be three parts.

**Cadmon in the Assembly** <note>original: Cadmon in Turba; referring to the "Turba Philosophorum" or "Assembly of the Philosophers," one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts.</note>:
> “From the right body take one part,”

namely of the entire material:

> “and three parts of the air,”

Here, even the air is taken for the perfect body, and that this is what is meant is evident from the fact that it is supposed to dissolve the ferment, as the whole material, and specifically into an equal water of Mercury; namely, the material should be elevated into a spiritual nature by the air; therefore, the three parts of the perfect

Some also call these three parts of air "three parts of pure gold," just as the *Sophic Water-Stone* <note>original: *Wasserstein der Weisen*; a famous 17th-century alchemical treatise attributed to Johann Isaac Hollandus or others.</note> does. Others call them an "unclean body" that should be digested and made pure with the <term>ferment</term> <note>A substance added to the alchemical mixture to "leaven" it and trigger transformation.</note> as with the material. **Sendivogius:** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist.</note> "Take the philosophical <term>menstruum</term> <note>original: *Menstruum*; a solvent capable of dissolving solids without altering their essential nature.</note> and put it in a vessel, so that three parts are empty and one part is filled with the material."

**Dornäus:** <note>Gerard Dorn (c. 1530–1584), a Belgian philosopher and defender of Paracelsus.</note> "In the weighing, one must proceed into infinity with two weights, one of which is one pound, but the other is three pounds."

In regard to increasing the power of the Stone, one proceeds through its entire process from the beginning into infinity, and there the weight of nature must be one part material and three parts air in the empty space of the glass.

**Sayings of Alanus:** <note>original: *Dicta Alani*; referring to Alanus ab Insulis, a 12th-century scholar to whom alchemical works were later attributed.</note> "Take three parts of Mercury <note>original: *Merkurii*</note> and one part of thinly beaten gold." Here, the entire material is taken to be the "thinly beaten gold."

**Paracelsus:** "Take Mercury <note>original: *Merkur*</note>, which has been purified seven times through saltpeter and alum, three pounds; and of Sal Ammoniac <note>original: *Salis Armoniaci*; ammonium chloride</note>, sublimated three times from clear salt of tartar <note>original: *Weinsteinsalz*; potassium carbonate</note>, one pound."

Here he indicates both the preliminary and subsequent work with their three and seven repetitions, and also the three parts of air in the vessel against one part of material; this measurement should remain exactly the same in all repetitions.

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

teachings <note>The text begins with the suffix "-rum," likely completing "Dictorum Alani" (The Sayings of Alanus).</note> can be unified into one in the rules of proportion, and can all be resolved through the proportions explained here. This author says:

> "Take two parts of the aforementioned water-matter," namely the <term>preliminary work</term> <note>original: *Vorarbeit*; the initial stages of cleaning or preparing the base substances.</note> and <term>follow-up work</term> <note>original: *Nacharbeit*; the final stages of refinement.</note>, "and from the same again three different parts," the three repetitions in the preliminary work, in which each time two parts of the water-matter are set to one part of <term>ferment</term>.

"Diligently preserve and keep both parts," which is done through the ferment. He hints at the ferment with the words where he says one must add a pure "gold body" as a ferment, specifically one part to twelve. These words appear differently in various books and, in themselves, do not actually belong to the proportion. However, if one takes the triple water-matter for the ferment, it makes four parts; and these, taken three times in the three repetitions, make twelve. This number twelve could be indicated by this, and nothing more. However, regarding the proportion of the ferment to the water-matter itself, he provides the following rhymes:

-> Seven and three are divided, <-
-> A half here, let no one hurry, <-
-> In sum, in this number so worthy, <-
-> Rest all things upon this earth. <-

The three and seven are the preliminary and follow-up work. The "half" is the ferment, for a ratio of one to two is the proportion upon which the entire <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> is built. Furthermore, he says:

> "Now when you have put both, water and gold, unequal parts, into a <term>dissolving dish</term> <note>original: *Solvirschale*; a vessel used for the chemical process of *solutio* or breaking down solids into liquid form.</note>, let them stand." The gold is the ferment, and that is the "half" mentioned in the rhymes—namely, the half taken against the water-matter—which is why both are unequal parts. This should be taken for the first <term>rotation</term> <note>original: *Rotation*; a full cycle of the alchemical process, often compared to the movement of the heavens.</note>,

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

The molten matter, which attracts the air, is two parts <term>Luna</term> <note>original: *Luna*; the alchemical name for silver</note>, and the <term>ferment</term> <note>an additive used to "ripen" or trigger the transformation of the matter</note> is one part <term>Sol</term> <note>original: *Sol*; the alchemical name for gold</note>. These are crushed and mixed together, through which the air is drawn in, and thus <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Merkur*; quicksilver, representing the fluid or "spirit" principle</note>, gold, and silver are prepared together. Because silver is twice as much as gold, the Mercury is also twice as much against gold as it is against silver—namely, four parts against gold and two against silver. For if the Mercury is two parts against the "animated" <term>corpus</term> <note>original: *Corpus*; the physical body or solid matter of the substance</note>, it must indeed be four parts against the ferment, because the ferment is only half as much as the body.

However, everything stands in a <term>triad</term> <note>original: *Dreyzahl*; a group of three</note>. The animated matter with the ferment is, as a whole body, a triad, because there are two parts silver and one part gold. Likewise, there are three parts of air in the empty space of the glass; and so there are three parts of the body, and three parts of the spirit. The matter animated with air is also called the "spiritual substance," while the ferment is to be regarded as the body; and thus there is also one part more of the spiritual substance than of the bodily. It is quite natural that all these parts must be taken in different considerations; for if the spiritual is to have three parts, and the bodily also three parts, then the spiritual cannot have one part more than the bodily.

<term>Philalethes</term> <note>original: *Philaletha*; referring to Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist</note> says in his explanation of these words:
> “Take your body, which without any doubtful speech is gold well-purified and made subtle, one part,”
namely <term>Tartar</term> <note>Though usually referring to potassium bitartrate, in this context it is a code name for the purified base material</note>:
> “and of our water, which without doubtful meaning is animated Mercury, which we then call our Luna, two parts, and mix it together into an <term>amalgam</term> <note>original: *Amalgama*; a blend of metals</note>.”
Further, <term>Ripley</term> <note>original: *Riplaus*; George Ripley, a 15th-century English alchemist</note> says:
> “But <term>Bacon</term> <note>referring to Roger Bacon, the 13th-century philosopher</note> took three parts of the spirit to one part of the body, for which sake I stayed awake many nights before I knew it; and both are correct, take whichever pleases you.”

The entire mass in the glass is the one part of the body, against three parts of air

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

...in the empty part of the glass. With these two external proportions—namely that of the <term>ferment</term> <note>a substance added to "ripen" or trigger the transformation of the base matter</note> against the <term>animated matter</term> <note>matter that has been "vitalized" or prepared through previous alchemical steps</note>, and of the total matter against the vessel—the philosophers play many games. This was also a specific lock placed before this art; for when proportion is discussed, one might think it refers to only one thing, yet it is not always the same. Furthermore:

> "If the water is equal in proportion to the earth, with the proper measure of heat, then a good new growth will spring from it, **white and red** <note>the *albedo* and *rubedo*; the two major successful stages of the alchemical Great Work</note>. Kill the living, and make the dead living. Make the <term>Trinity</term> <note>the three alchemical principles of Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt</note> into Unity without resistance. This is the safest and best proportion, for the less of the spiritual part there is, the better the **solution** <note>the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note> will be. But if you did it with too much water, your earth would be drowned and everything spoiled. Never suffocate your earth with water."

These equal parts, earth and water, are precisely what were previously called the three parts spirit and three parts body. In number, both are equal because both stand in the number three, yet they are not equal in quantity. When such three parts are in the matter and also in the empty space of the glass, and the fire is then correct—so that the internal proportion is also achieved—a new growth must come from it. This triad above and below shall become one, without resistance; from this, it follows that the "equal parts" of earth and water are not meant literally. For the less there is of the spiritual part (as water), the better the solution is, and one should never take too much water—that is, it should never draw in too much air. Therefore, the equality of water and earth must indeed have another meaning. This is merely a **play of wit** <note>original: *Lusus ingenii*; a clever intellectual game or puzzle</note> with the numbers of proportions, as they mix one into the other and perform various contemplations by comparing one...

<meta>catchword: with</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the symbolic and numerical ratios in alchemy, specifically the "Trinity" of spirit and body. It warns that "water" (the spiritual/liquid principle) must be carefully balanced so as not to "drown" the "earth" (the physical matter).</summary>

# On the Proportion of the Materials.

...with the other, and as long as one does not fully internalize and understand this, one has nothing but loss, sorrow, and distress as a reward for all one's trouble.

> Item, **Ripley** <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), an influential English alchemist</note>: "You must take three parts of the **white woman** <note>mercury or the feminine principle</note>, and one part of the **red man** <note>sulfur or the masculine principle</note>: or two parts against one part, according to **Raymond** <note>Raymond Llull</note>: or four to one, according to **Alan** <note>Alanus ab Insulis</note>: or three against one. For your <term>compound</term> <note>the combined alchemical mixture</note>, make the <term>ferment</term> <note>the catalytic substance, like yeast, used to 'ripen' the work</note>, which must be gold or silver, the fourth part."

Three to one part is always the proportion of the three parts of air in the vessel against one part of the matter: however, it is also the triple water against the one ferment, which is why this proportion appears here twice. Two parts to one part is the ordinary proportion of two parts of the <term>animated matter</term> <note>matter that has been 'vitalized' or prepared through previous alchemical steps</note> against one part of the ferment. Four parts to one is nothing other than the three parts of air in the vessel, taken together with the animated matter as one part—which together makes four—against the one part of ferment. And the ferment as the fourth part is the total matter against the three parts of air in the vessel, for the matter is the ferment of the spirit; and this ferment is silver, from the beginning of the <term>subsequent work</term> <note>the later stages of the alchemical process</note> until the end of the third <term>rotation</term> <note>a complete cycle of distillation or heating</note>, when the <term>tincture</term> <note>the transformative essence</note> for silver is finished. From then until the end, the matter is the ferment of gold, because at the end the gold tincture is present.

### §. 9.

> **Rhasis** <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (865–925), a Persian physician and alchemist</note>: "Our Stone shares its name with the Creator of the world, who is a Trinity and a Unity. Therefore **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> says: The confidence of the philosophers stands in two things, to which they add the third. These same philosophers have also called it an <term>Imparem</term> <note>original Latin: *imparem*; meaning an odd or uneven number</note> or an uneven thing."

The number three is the banner and the standard of the Philosophers' Stone, and the Stone bears the name of the Creator of the world. Our God, Jesus Christ, calls himself a stone, the cornerstone...

...stone and touchstone; yet it is not a stone for that reason, but rather it is the eternal God, and His eternal essence and spirit within all nature and every creature. However, it is no stone, though many fools have bashed their heads against it; rather, it is the life of the world and the external spirit of every creature.

Three times three is nine, and the number nine is the concluding number. With the conclusion of this time, this Stone—this fruit of Paradise—reveals itself openly before the eyes of all the world and calls out with a loud voice:

Oh, how blessed is he who has a share in this resurrection from the dead; and oh, what a pity for him who must look on from behind, even if the whole world with all its kingdoms were his own,

->### The Harmony of the Stone with Christ and the World.<-

The **Tartar** <note>Potassium bitartrate, an acidic salt found in wine casks; in alchemy, it represents the "fixed" or grounded principle</note> is called the **Sun** and the **Saltpeter** <note>Potassium nitrate; in alchemy, it represents the "volatile" or active, celestial principle</note> is called the **Moon**; just so is the **Old Testament** <note>original: "A." for *Altes Testament*</note> the Sun and the **New Testament** <note>original: "N." for *Neues Testament*</note> the Moon. The Tartar and the Old Testament have the color **red** as their sign, and the Saltpeter and the New Testament have the color **white**.

The Tartar is the main ground and the main essence in the **Stone**, and it must ground, ferment, and strengthen everything, and is therefore also taken doubly in the first foundation of the Stone. And just as in the preliminary work Tartar is always added, until the end of the process, through which everything becomes a pure Tartar-ground, in which the saltpetrous nature lies submerged; just so has the spirit of revelation in the Christian church for a long time pointed pious souls more and more to the Old Testament, and has many

### The Harmony of the Stone with the World.

...but cannot yet see or understand, he will nonetheless be able to see what he can grasp with his hands: namely, that all coarse physical things on Earth are divided into **three kingdoms**—the mineral, vegetable, and animal kingdoms. The Church likewise exists in three dispensations <note>original: "Haushaltungen"; referring to the theological eras of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</note>, and man in three primary parts: **body, soul, and spirit**. In the same way, the **Stone** possesses the number three in its three principles, the number three in the air within the vessel, in the composition of materials, and in the **three rotations** <term>rotations</term> <note>repeated cycles of distillation or heating in the alchemical process</note>.

There are also **four** primary natures of the outer world—if the satanic nature, which also had to serve the creation, is included—and there are **four elements** which constitute nature. There are also four primary religions in the Church, and our Earth has divided itself into four primary quarters of the world. Just so, the Stone has four parts when the **tartar-ferment** <term>tartar-ferment</term> <note>a catalytic substance derived from potassium bitartrate, used here to "leaven" or transform the matter</note> is added to the triple water.

If the aforementioned number four of the Stone is taken three times according to the three rotations in the preparatory work, there are **twelve parts** which constitute the entire Stone in its preparation; these are substantially present and are not mere mental images, for the four parts in each rotation are substantially present, and in each one they differ in power from the others. In the same way, the entire heaven, or the entire circle of the world, is divided into **twelve celestial signs** <note>the Zodiac</note>, and nature itself has twelve semitones in music. Therefore, the twelve celestial signs are also contained within the riddles of the Sages regarding the Stone.

The world also has **seven planets**; nature has **seven tones** or steps in music, within which the previously mentioned twelve semitones lie; and it also has **seven metals** among the physical things on Earth. In the same way, the Stone has **seven repetitions** in the finishing work <note>original: "Nacharbeit"; the final stages of refinement following the initial preparation</note>. ### The Harmony of the Size of the Parts of the Stone with the World in its Parts.

->§ 13.<-

There is nothing in the world without a natural basis and cause for being as it is; therefore, the sizes of the circles and divisions of the world are also not without basis and cause for being as they are. These causes, however, lie in the nature of the four elements themselves, which cannot be other than they are. When we observe the parts of the world, we find more of the upper and subtle than of the lower and coarser, and we find this same thing in the Stone. There is more seawater than dry land, and likewise there is more water-matter in the Stone than of the <term>tartar-ferment</term> <note>original: "Tartar-Ferment"; the acidic sediment or "lees" used as a catalyst to trigger transformation</note>. The circle of air is larger than the circle of the corporeal earth, and likewise the empty space filled with air in the <term>glass</term> <note>the alchemical vessel or retort</note> is larger than the corporeal matter of the Stone. But if one compares the Stone with the whole world, the <term>tartar</term> <note>potassium bitartrate, representing the earthly foundation or "salt"</note> resembles the whole lower world; the body animated by air resembles the planetary space up—

<header>The Harmony of the Stone with the World.</header>

...[larger] than our lower circle; and the empty space in the glass filled with air resembles the sphere of the fixed stars <note>In alchemy, the "glass" or flask is viewed as a miniature universe; the upper part of the vessel represents the heavens where the subtle vapors circulate</note>.

These are not empty speculations or images without substance, but are pure reality and the essence itself. For we see, both in the world and in the Stone, that there is more of the upper, more perfect, and more subtle than of the lower, more imperfect, and coarser; and that the upper occupies a larger space than the lower. We see that the upper is more active than the lower, and that the upper is the life and movement of the lower. We see that the upper is more perfect than the lower, and that the lower must be improved and made perfect through the upper. All of this is thus based on the ground of the Godhead and Nature, and it can and must not be otherwise than it is, because otherwise nothing—not even Holy God Himself—could exist. For if the upper and perfect were not larger, and if there were not more of it than of the lower and imperfect, then blessing and goodness could not penetrate from the upper into the lower. The perfect must help that which lies in imperfection; therefore it must be larger and more powerful. The perfect must set limits on the corruption within the imperfect, [dictating] how far it should go; therefore it must be more powerful and must have a larger scope of operation with the pure natures, so that it can keep the corruption in the lower within its bounds and, in due time, root it out entirely.

The whole world works together in all its parts as one, and the Earth is the point toward which all effects of the whole world are directed, for the creation of all that God wishes to have created. For the sake of creation, the parts of the world were made, and our Earth is the body of the world which God chose for creation; He placed it in such imperfection for this purpose, because the creation of man in abundance <note>original: "Menge"; here referring to the vast number or multiplicity of physical beings</note> could not occur in a state of [static] perfection.

# The Harmony of the Stone with the World.

The main parts into which the world has been set, into those very same parts has the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: Stein. Refers to the Philosopher's Stone, the aim of the alchemical Great Work.</note> also been set; and in both there are two main parts, namely the Upper and the Lower, Male and Female, through which all things are brought forth. In the great world <note>the macrocosm or universe</note>, the upper powers sink down into the lower powers, and the lower nature binds, <term>coagulates</term> <note>term: Coagulate (coaguliret). To change from a fluid to a solid or semi-solid state; in alchemy, this is the process of fixing a volatile spirit into a stable material form.</note> and thickens the upper powers, for which even the hellish <term>Tartarus</term> <note>term: Tartarus. In classical mythology, the deepest abyss of the underworld. Here, it represents the "satanic" or chaotic, dark aspect of matter that must be overcome or utilized in the process of purification.</note>, the satanic nature, must assist at the same time. In the same way, the upper powers of the air are sunk into the lower part of the Stone and are <term>coagulated</term> within it. And just as these powers of the air improve and exalt the lower matter itself—namely, by setting the parts of the Stone into their right proportion so that none may destroy the other, and by then sealing the Stone against the access of the outer air, wherein lies corruption and incessant change—even so will God improve and exalt the entire lower nature when He sets its parts in the earth and seas, along with the atmosphere, into another and better proportion toward one another, and binds the satanic nature.

The <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>original: Stein der Weisen.</note> is therefore the most perfect image and likeness of the creation and formation of the world, of the Church, and of all things; and it is also primarily the image of the restoration and restitution <note>original: Wiederbringung. A reference to the doctrine of "Apokatastasis," the belief that all of creation will eventually be restored to a state of divine perfection.</note> of all things into their first glory, in which they were initially created. Herein the Stone calls out to us and says:
> Behold, this is our God, for whom we wait, and He will help us. This is the Jehovah for whom we wait, that we may rejoice and be glad in His salvation; for the hand of Jehovah rests upon this mountain, it rests upon this stone. Isaiah 25:9. <note>The author has slightly adapted the biblical text to include the word "stone," linking scripture directly to the alchemical work.</note>

Behold, I make all things new! And He says to me:
> Write, for these words are true and certain. Revelation 21:5.

# Chapter Six

## The Preparation of the Philosopher’s Stone in Preliminary and Subsequent Work.

### Process of the Stone.

->§. 1.<-

Take two parts of **crystals of tartar**, and one part of pure **saltpeter** or Indian saltpeter. Let them flow together and melt well in a crucible over a strong charcoal fire. Pour it out into a clean mortar; thus one has a white salt, which now attracts the air. As soon as it has become hard enough to be crushed, proceed as follows: First, have glass **carafes** <note>original: *Caravinen*; these are small, long-necked glass flasks used in chemical operations.</note> at hand, of such a size that if one puts one and a half ounces of the crushed matter into them, then only a fourth part of the vessel is filled, and three parts remain empty; this can first be tested with plain tartar alone. One must also have tartar at hand, well-crushed into a fine powder <note>original: *Mehl*; literally "flour" or "meal," referring to a very finely ground substance.</note>, to serve as a **ferment** <note>In alchemy, a ferment is a substance added to a base to "leaven" or trigger a specific chemical transformation, similar to how yeast works in baking.</note> at the ready.

If one is to put, for example, nine <term>quintals</term> <note>original: *Quent* or *Quentchen*; a historical German unit of weight equal to approximately 3.6 grams or 1/4 of a Loth.</note> into a carafe, then crush six quintals of the melted <term>mass</term> <note>original: *Massa*</note> into a very fine powder, and mix three quintals of plain crushed crystals of tartar into it as a ferment; and do not delay in this. Then seal it in the vessel and close it well with a stopper and wax around it, so that not the slightest bit of air can enter further; and let this <term>composition</term> <note>original: *Compositum*</note> stand still for ninety days.

At the end of ninety days, open it and remove three quintals of the mass, and [add] again three quintals of plain [crushed]—

<summary>This page introduces the practical recipe for the Philosopher's Stone, beginning with the creation of a white salt from tartar and saltpeter and the start of a ninety-day incubation period. It emphasizes precise measurements and the exclusion of air to ensure the purity of the chemical composition.</summary>
<keywords>crystals of tartar, saltpeter, crucible, carafes, quintals, ferment, ninety-day process, alchemical preparation</keywords>

# The Harmony of the Stone with the World.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

...materials <note>The text begins mid-word; likely *Materien* (materials/matters).</note> of the Stone can be made, and that they must be proportioned against each other in such and such a manner.

Therefore, this art of making the **Philosopher’s Stone** comes from our God and Father **Adam** himself. Just as the world was made, so too is the Stone made; and just as the world could not have been made in any other way than it was actually made, so also the Stone can be made in no other way than it is described here, and there is only a single way to make it. There is only one God, and so also only one Stone. The Creator who made the world also established the arrangement of the Stone in its parts according to the very same laws by which He made the world. Therefore, the Stone bears the name of the Creator of the world. Is this not the world as it stands before our eyes, and as I have described it? Who can say, "No, that is not it"? So it is also with the Philosopher’s Stone. Who would deny this Stone and say, "That is not it," since it truly stands in the figure of the creation of the world? 

No <term>adept</term> <note>original: *Adeptus*; a master who has successfully completed the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note> has ever been able to show such a harmony of the Stone with the creation of the world, with the entire structure of the universe, with the whole Church, and with **Jesus Christ** <note>original: *Jesu Christo*</note>, as happens in this book; and the mystery of the Stone has never been revealed to anyone in its full breadth until now. But if, notwithstanding all this, a <term>sophist</term> <note>In alchemical literature, a "sophist" refers to a false practitioner or a "charcoal burner" who follows deceptive logic or recipes without true spiritual or chemical understanding.</note> should cry out against it and say: "That is not the Philosopher’s Stone," then let this first voice of his be a sign that he understands nothing, is no adept, but lies buried only in sophistic opinions. However, it is only proper for an honest man not to reject or criticize a thing until he has first tested it properly and correctly and found it to be false. In short, let this Stone in its revelation be the herald that announces the coming of its Lord who created it. Whoever chooses not to heed it, does so at his own peril.

<summary>The author argues that the creation of the Philosopher's Stone is a divine art originating with Adam, mirroring the exact laws and proportions God used to create the universe. It positions the Stone as a physical manifestation of the macrocosm and warns that only those who physically test the process—rather than skeptical "sophists"—can judge its truth.</summary>

# The Sixth Chapter.

## The Preparation of the Philosophers' Stone in Preliminary and Sequential Work.

### Process of the Stone.

->§ 1.<-

"Take two parts of crystals of tartar <note>original: *Tartarkrystallen*; potassium bitartrate, often derived from wine barrels.</note>, and one part of pure saltpeter or Indian saltpeter. Let them flow together and melt well in a crucible <term>schmelztiegel</term> <note>original: *Schmelztiegel*; a ceramic or metal container used for high-temperature melting.</note> over a strong charcoal fire. Pour it out into a clean mortar; thus one has a white salt, which now attracts the air <note>This indicates the substance is hygroscopic, meaning it absorbs moisture from the atmosphere.</note>. As soon as it has become hard enough to be ground, proceed as follows: First, have glass carafes <term>caravinen</term> <note>original: *Caravinen*; specific glass flasks used in laboratory work.</note> at hand that are large enough so that when one puts one and a half ounces <term>unzen</term> <note>original: *Unzen*; a historical unit of weight.</note> of the ground material into them, then only a fourth part of the vessel is filled, and three parts remain empty; this can first be tested with plain tartar alone. One must also have at hand tartar, well ground into a fine powder, to serve as the ferment.

"If one is now to put, for example, nine quent <term>quent</term> <note>original: *Quent* or *Quentchen*; a small unit of weight, approximately 3.6 grams or 1/4 of a 'Lot'.</note> into a carafe, then grind six quent of the melted mass <term>massa</term> <note>original: *Massa*.</note> into a very fine powder, and mix three quent of plain ground crystals of tartar into it well as a ferment, and do not delay in doing so. Then seal it in the vessel and close it well with a stopper and wax around it, so that not the slightest bit of air can enter further, and let this compound <term>compositum</term> <note>original: *Compositum*.</note> stand quietly for ninety days.

"At the end of ninety days, open it and remove three quent of the mass, and [add] again three quent of plain grou... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

> "When it has passed through three times forty days, or three rotations, one then has the Universal Medicine <term>universalmedicin</term> in its first degree, and the tincture for silver; but at the end of the seventh rotation, one has it for gold, according to the form of the seven congregations in Revelation <note>original: *Apocal.*, referring to the Book of Revelation.</note>, where the white stone is promised to the third congregation, and the gold to the seventh."

Is the Stone then not the true image of Jesus Christ? Is it not His likeness and image in external nature? Is it not, in its creation and formation, like the creation of the world, the church, and man? Certainly; therefore it is the stone with seven eyes, which Jehovah placed before the face of Joshua <note>A biblical reference to Zechariah 3:9: "For behold the stone that I have laid before Joshua; upon one stone shall be seven eyes."</note>, as an external, visible testimony that He has likewise given him the spiritual stone, Jesus Christ, together with...

### §. 2.

This little recipe is thus the entire matter of the great mystery of the Philosopher's Stone <term>stein der weisen</term>. It is true, to the outward eyes it is unsightly, and he who only looks at the shell of things will not be able to believe quickly that this is the Philosopher's Stone. However, it has pleased the Most High to hide the highest and best under an unsightly form. He has hidden Himself, as the spiritual Stone, under the name *Adam*; but the natural stone He has placed and hidden in these unremarkable things, as a testimony that He rejects that which appears high and shining in this world, and instead chooses that which appears lowly and despised. And who will be able to believe that our father *Adam*, the true *Jesus Christ*, the one true God and eternal life, is he, unless he has spiritual eyes of true understanding from the divine light?

But do we not read of *Jesus Christ* that He was despised in His state of humiliation? As *Adam* He was on earth in His state of humiliation, and no one regarded Him. Can it be any different with the *Messiah* in the end? Just so is the compound <term>compositum</term> <note>original: *Compositum*; the alchemical mixture or substance being worked upon in the vessel.</note> of the Stone despised in this state of humiliation, although it is already ensouled and quickened with the Spirit of the World. A fool, who cannot see with the inner eyes of understanding and cannot direct his sight toward spiritual natures, will mock it. They are numbered among those who now reject the revelation of Jesus Christ, who stumble, are ensnared and captured, and who deny the true God—the Rock from which they all have come.

I have already said that the purpose of the Stone is not to make gold and silver, even though it can indeed do such things, and one could use it for that purpose with benefit in times past. I do not wish to teach how to make gold—for what use would that be on the Day of the Lord, which is now so near?—but rather, I wish to reveal its secret, both to strike down the idols of gold and silver and render these metals worthless, and also to prove the mysteries of God in Nature through the Stone, and to bring to light what God intends to teach us through the Philosopher's Stone. However, so that one may further see that this is the true Philosopher's Stone, let us now hear the teachings of the Sages regarding its preparation.

### §. 4.

The method of teaching the preparation of the Stone through images and riddles is such that it often combines the preliminary work <term>preliminary work</term><note>German: *Vorarbeit*. In alchemical practice, this usually refers to the initial purification and preparation of the "First Matter."</note> and the subsequent work <term>subsequent work</term><note>German: *Nacharbeit*. no one can work who does not yet truly understand the matter, and thus is not yet an <term>Adept Philosopher</term><note>original: "Philosophus Adeptus." A title for a practitioner who has successfully mastered the secrets of alchemy.</note>. Furthermore:

From this passage, it can also be seen that the Sages understand the preparatory work itself as the "purification of their materials." > "key you have opened the first lock."

He has certainly not yet unlocked everything with this, for the ferment is still missing, as is the time required for a <term>rotation</term><note>original: "Rotation." A cyclical alchemical process, often involving repeated distillation or circulation of matter in a sealed vessel.</note>.

They also call the <term>preparatory work</term><note>original: "Vorarbeit." > „just what the field is to the grain of wheat,“ for in the air
> the tartar grows in strength, just as the grain of wheat grows in the field.

> „of common gold.“

A hypothetical universal essence believed by alchemists to be the source of life and transmutation.</note> with the <term>Salt</term><note>original: "Salz." This is the whole mass against the three parts of air in the empty space of the glass, and it is also the <term>Tartar-Ferment</term><note>original: "Tartar-Ferment." A substance derived from wine-sediment (tartar) used to trigger the transformation of the metal.</note> against the triple water, as the matter animated with air.

Alchemical shorthand for the moisture that condenses and runs down the sides of the glass vessel during heating.</note> may circulate for ninety days and nights; so you will see that the common gold has been transformed into our Gold by the Mercury."

A symbolic term for completing a full cycle of the alchemical process.</note>. # The Preparation of the Stone.

“This <term>Sulfur</term><note>original: "Schwefel." In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle of matter, not necessarily the chemical element sulfur.</note> is our sulfur,” namely the mass at the end of the preparatory work, “but it does not yet tinge <note>original: "tingiret." From "tincture," referring to the power of the Philosopher's Stone to transform or "dye" base metals into gold.</note>: and believe me, many philosophers have worked in this way and have attained the truth. But you must not imagine, once you have attained this sulfur, that you then possess the Stone; no, but rather only its true matter.” This man’s wisdom is quite lovely, for he indicates the end of the preparatory work with it, yet in such a way that the inexperienced person could not think otherwise than that the very first beginning of the work must start here.

->§. 6.<-

Also: “If you work in common gold,” preparatory work, “then that is not strictly our work, and yet it will lead you directly to our work at its appointed time. But if you work in common gold, then see to it that you bring about <term>Venus's Marriage</term><note>original: "Veneris Heyrath." A symbolic term for the union of chemical principles; Venus often represents copper or the feminine, receptive principle.</note> with great care,” that is, the pleasure—as the Wise Men's <term>Venus</term>—must unify properly with the matter and dry out. “Then repeat the work with that <term>Mercury</term> which is called <term>Virgin's Milk</term><note>original: "Jungfernmilch." A traditional alchemical term for a white, nourishing solvent or a specific stage of distilled liquid used to "feed" the matter.</note>.”

This same <term>Philalethes</term><note>original: "Philaletha." Referring to Eirenaeus Philalethes, the pseudonym of a famous 17th-century alchemical author whose works were highly influential.</note> indicates the entire preparation of the Stone and its duration as follows:

“If you work with the Mercury I have described, and in the purest common gold with proper fire, you will find our Gold within seven or at most nine months, and our Silver in five months, and that is the true appointed time to manufacture this sulfur; if you take it to be our finished work, you are still in error. Although from this, through repeated labor with only perceptible fire, you will attain the true <term>Elixir</term><note>original: "Elixir." The final, perfected form of the Stone, capable of transmutation and healing.</note>, and all this in a year and a half, with God's help, to whom be glory for eternity.”

# The Preparation of the Stone.

The nine months are the actual time of the preparatory work <note>original: "Vorarbeit." In alchemy, this refers to the initial purification of materials before the final "great work."</note>. The seven months and also the five months mean nothing; they are only companions to the number nine, used to somewhat conceal it and not name it all too clearly on its own. That this is the end of the preparatory work follows from the ninety days and the "turning of the wheel three times," which he expressly calls the work in common gold using the word <term>Riplai</term> <note>original: "Riplai." This refers to George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist whose works, like *The Compound of Alchymie*, were highly influential.</note>. Ninety days taken three times are nine months. The mass brought to this point is what he calls the finished <term>Sulfur</term>, the finished <term>Stone</term>—which, however, does not yet tinge <note>To "tinge" means to transform or dye base metals into gold.</note>—and the prepared gold of the wise, since it has been brought back into its first matter. But now he says: "Once you have prepared our gold, seal it for forty days etc." <note>original: "x." used as an abbreviation for "etc."</note> and here he says that through repeated work on this substance over a year and a half, one attains the true <term>Elixir</term>. A year and a half are two times nine months; and the forty days taken seven times in the follow-up work <note>original: "Nacharbeit." The second major phase of the process, involving maturation and multiplication.</note> also make nine months, which, together with the nine months of the preparatory work, make up the eighteen months or a year and a half.

In this way, this excellent man has described and said everything quite precisely, yet has nonetheless concealed it with beautiful, lovely wisdom. No one has spoken more clearly than he, but no one was permitted to say the matter even more clearly. For what would have happened if gold- and silver-making had become generally known? The world could not have endured in its previous state—in which it nevertheless had to and must endure—for we were supposed to and had to stand under the curse and under idols. The gold and silver idols had to rule; for if these metals had not possessed this value, they could not have held the bond of human society together, since one man still has to lend a hand to another for the sake of money. Therefore, before the coming of <term>Elias</term> <note>original: "Elias." Likely referring to Elias Artista (Elijah the Artist), a legendary figure in alchemy prophesied by Paracelsus to reveal all secrets before the end of the world.</note> no one was allowed to reveal the stone, but Elias

# The Preparation of the Stone.

The preliminary stage of purifying the base materials.</note>, "but it does not yet <term>tinge</term> <note>To "tinge" (from Latin *tingere*) means to transform or dye a base metal into gold or silver.</note>: and believe me, many philosophers have worked in this way and have attained the truth. But you must not imagine, once you have obtained this sulfur, that you then possess the Stone; no, rather only its true matter." ### §. 6.

Also: "If you work in common gold," the preparatory work, "that is actually not our work, and yet at its determined time it will lead you straight to our work. But if you work in common gold, see to it that you bring about the <term>Marriage of Venus</term> <note>original: "Veneris Heyrath." A symbolic union of alchemical principles; here interpreted as the "desire" or attraction within the matter.</note> with great care," that is, the desire, as the Venus of the Wise, must rightly unite with the matter and dry out. A common alchemical term for a specific solvent or mercurial water used to dissolve or nourish the "Stone."</note>."

> "If you will work in the Mercury I have described, and in the purest common gold with appropriate fire, you will find our gold within seven or at most nine months, and our silver in five months; and that is the true determined time to manufacture this sulfur, which, if you take it for our finished work, you are still in error. Although from this, through repeated work with only sensible fire <note>original: "empfindlichen Feuer." Usually refers to a gentle, steady heat that can be felt but is not destructive, often compared to the heat of a brooding hen.</note>, you will obtain the true Elixir, and all of that in a year and a half, with God’s help, to whom be glory in eternity."

# The Preparation of the Stone.

The nine months are the actual time of the preparatory work <note>original: "Vorarbeit." The preliminary stage of purifying and preparing the base materials.</note>. The seven months and also the five mean nothing, but are only companions to the number nine, in order to somewhat conceal it and not name it all too clearly on its own. That this is the end of the preparatory work follows from the ninety days and the threefold turning of the wheel, which he explicitly calls the work in common gold using the words of <term>Ripläi</term> <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), an influential English alchemist and author of *The Compound of Alchymy*.</note>; however, ninety days taken three times are nine months. But now he says: When you have prepared our gold, then shut it up for forty days, etc.; and here he says that through repeated work on this, one obtains the true elixir in a year and a half. A year and a half is twice nine months, and the forty days taken seven times in the follow-up work <note>original: "Nacharbeit." The subsequent refinement process after the initial preparation.</note> also make nine months; these, together with the nine months of the preparatory work, constitute the eighteen months or a year and a half.

In this way, this excellent man described and said everything quite precisely, yet still concealed it with beautiful, lovely wisdom. No one spoke more clearly than he, but no one was permitted to say the matter even more clearly; for what would have happened if the making of gold and silver had become generally known? The world could not have continued in its current state, in which it nevertheless should and had to exist, for we should and had to stand under the curse and under the idols. The gold and silver idols had to rule; for if these metals had not possessed this value, they could not have held the bond of human society together, since one man has had to lend a hand to another for the sake of money. Therefore, before <term>Elias</term> <note>Referring to "Elias Artista" (Elias the Artist), a legendary alchemical figure predicted by Paracelsus who would appear in the "last days" to reveal all secrets of the Great Work.</note>, no one was allowed to reveal the Stone, but Elias must

must do it, because with it this world and time shall come to an end, and all idols shall be rooted out.

The Sages call the preparatory work <note>original: "Vorarbeit." The initial phase of purifying the raw materials.</note> that has been brought to its conclusion their **gold**, and they then often confuse this gold with the actual gold at the end of the process in order to once again conceal the matter. Therefore, **Philaletha** <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a 17th-century alchemist (likely George Starkey) whose works were highly influential.</note> says further:

> "Know then, that this alone makes the books difficult to understand, 
> "because they all teach of one governance in various ways: 
> "and when they speak of one work, they teach the 
> "governance of the other, in which net I was long entangled, 
> "before I could loosen my feet from the snare."

### Of the Eagles of the Sages.

->§. 7.<-

The Sages also divide their work into three, seven, nine, and ten eagles. But their eagle, as the <term>Vogel Hermetis</term> <note>Latin: "Bird of Hermes." A common alchemical symbol for Mercury or the volatile spirit that "flies" or rises during distillation.</note>, is **mercury**, the air. In the preparatory work there are three eagles, which, together with the seven in the follow-up work, make ten eagles. And because there must be three parts of air in the empty space of the vessel, in the preparatory work they are taken as three times three, making nine eagles. The three in the preparatory work and the seven in the follow-up work, which together make ten, make thirty when taken three times; and these are the **thirty days** in which the true <term>Wine of the Sages</term> <note>A symbolic name for the alchemical solvent or the transformed matter in its liquid state.</note> is prepared, as they often teach. The last seven repetitions taken three times are the twenty-one <term>Rotationes</term> <note>Latin: "Rotations." These are cycles of heating, evaporation, and condensation (distillation) intended to purify the substance.</note> or revolutions, which some also mention.

Some also say the work is completed in three days, or even in three months, by which they mean the three rotations in the first work. Others say in four months, when the follow-up work is included as one. Still others say in seven, nine, or ten days, which—in the manner previously mentioned—

The two main phases of the alchemical process.</note> from one another. ->§. 8.<-

Regarding the eagles, **Philaletha** says: "The Mercury of the Sages is the <term>Bird of Hermes</term> <note>original: "Vogel Hermetis." A symbol for mercury or the volatile spirit that "flies" upward during heating.</note>. But when the Sages speak of their eagle in the plural, they set the number from three to ten, namely three in the first work, and seven in the other." He solves this riddle thus: One should take four parts of the **fiery dragon**, **saltpeter**, and nine parts of the **magnet**, **tartar**, and melt these together, which is the work on the **coal fire**. This should be purified three times, which are the three **rotations** in the preparatory work. Then, he says—after many things mixed in for concealment—when the **moon** shines in its full light, that is, when the preparatory work, which is called the **moist moon**, is at an end, then give it feathers, that is, make it **volatile** <note>original: "flüchtig." In chemistry, this means a substance that evaporates easily.</note> in the follow-up work. Repeat this seven times, then you will come to rest. See with what lovely wisdom this man has described the entire work!

Of the whole work he says: "I swear to you, by word and faith, if this were openly revealed to the light of day, even the fools would laugh at the art," for when one knows this, namely the arrangement, "everything is nothing but a **work of women** and **play of children** <note>A traditional alchemical expression meaning that once the chemical secret is understood, the remaining work is as simple as household chores like cooking or cleaning.</note>, that is, only cooking. Therefore the Sages have hidden this secret in the highest and most artful way, and

# The Preparation of the Stone. 120

"Believe firmly that we have also done so." Oh yes, you dear **Philaletha** <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist whose identity remained a secret.</note>, you have indeed hidden it with much wisdom and prudence, and yet revealed it! Here we also see the confession of this man, that it is a poor, unsightly thing, such that one would not think this could be the **Philosopher’s Stone**. The fools, who want to be far too clever and have only high and shiny things in their heads, would only have laughed at it.

When Philaletha distinguishes the first and second work from each other, or compares both; he also calls the first the **Regiment of Mercury** <note>original: "Regiment des Merkurs." A phase characterized by moisture and volatility.</note>, because it is more moist than the second. The completed mass of the **preparatory work** <note>original: "Vorarbeit."</note> is also called **Lead**, or **Saturn**, because it afterwards attracts the air without further addition, perfects itself, and becomes, as it were, gold from lead. When this mass enters the **follow-up work** <note>original: "Nacharbeit."</note>, Philaletha calls it the **Regiment of Saturn**, and says: "You shall know that our lead is more precious than all gold. It is the **clay** <note>original: "Letten." Usually refers to a fatty clay or loam; here it signifies the "earthy" base of the stone.</note> in which the soul of gold is united with mercury."

## The Fountain of Count Bernard.

### §. 9.

It is lovely to the eyes to see the light of the suns, for the sun of wisdom is manifold and yet one, as one sees in the riddles of the **Philosopher's Stone**, where each person has exercised themselves in wisdom according to their gift, to depict one and the same thing under manifold images.

The air, which is added to the matter in the follow-up work, is also called the **King's Grave**, and they also call this work in the beginning **Saturn**. Count **Bernard** <note>Bernard Trevisan (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist famous for his allegorical stories of kings and fountains.</note>, however, calls the follow-up work in regard to the air the **Fountain** <note>original: "Fontina." # The Preparation of the Stone. 121

[the ki]ng went to bathe, and he calls the **matter of the stone** <note>original: "Materie des Steins."</note> the "golden booklet," which he let fall into the **fountain** <note>original: "Fontina." A reference to the chemical bath or solvent in the vessel.</note>, for the **mass** <note>original: "Massa."</note> brought to completion in the **preliminary work** <note>original: "Vorarbeit."</note> is their gold, which goes into this fountain for the **follow-up work** <note>original: "Nacharbeit."</note>. He says:

> “When one has begun to awaken the heat in this bath of the **fountain**
> “to bathe the king, he had to remain in **prison** <note>original: "Gefängniß." In alchemy, this refers to the sealed vessel (the Hermetic Seal) where the matter is confined.</note> for forty days.”

These are the forty days of a **rotation** <note>original: "Rotation." A cyclical process of heating, evaporation, and condensation.</note>, which he also indicates with the following words:

> “When the king went into the bath, he took off his robe woven with
> “**gold leaf** and handed it to **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturno." In this context, Saturn represents the "nigredo" or black stage, the beginning of the work associated with lead.</note>, who kept it for forty days.”

The golden robe is the matter itself, and the first rotation in the follow-up work they call **Saturn**, from which there are seven rotations until the **Sun** <note>The "Sun" represents the final stage of perfection and the color red.</note>, which indicates the **gold** at the end. He also says: The entire time the king remained in the bath was 282 days, which is seven times forty days <note>While 7 x 40 is 280, the author likely accounts for a short transitional period.</note>.

Likewise he says: “When the king has resolved to go into the “bath; he leaves all his **courtiers** <note>original: "Hofgesinde." Symbolic of impurities or secondary substances.</note> and all strangers behind, “and goes alone into the **fountain**, and no one may approach “it except him alone and his keeper; and this keeper is a “very lowly man, and even the simplest person on earth “can perform this duty, because he does nothing more than “warm the bath.”

That the king goes into the bath so entirely alone and without strangers indicates that in the follow-up work no addition is made to the matter, and that it goes into the **air-bath** <note>original: "Luftbad."</note> alone. The watchman who warms the bath is the **artist** <note>The alchemist himself.</note> himself, who opens the glass at the end of each rotation so that fresh air may enter. This opening and re-stopping could certainly be performed by the simplest person on earth. Further:

<meta>This page continues the interpretation of the Parable of the Fountain. The previous page discussed the initial forty-day "prison" of the king. This page explains the subsequent stages or "rotations" of the alchemical process, transitioning from the blackening associated with Saturn to the whitening stage of the Moon.</meta>

# The Preparation of the Stone. 122

> “When the king has entered the **small fountain** <note>original: "Fontina." A Latin term for a small fountain, representing the alchemical vessel or the solvent in which the matter is dissolved.</note>, and has been locked in by the keeper, he does not appear again until after 130 days, when he emerges very bright and shining.”

These are the three times forty days <note>Though the quote mentions 130 days, the author interprets this as three cycles of forty days (120 days total); such numerical discrepancies were often used in alchemical texts to "blind" or hide secrets from the uninitiated.</note>, with which he indicates the time until the **white tincture** <term>White Tincture: A substance capable of transmuting base metals into silver, representing the "Albedo" or whitening stage of the Great Work.</term> upon silver. The fact that the king no longer appears until then simply means that the **matter** <note>original: "Materie."</note> neither should nor may be taken out for any profit or use until then. Further:

> “When the king goes into the bath, he takes off his **golden garment** and gives it to his first chamberlain, **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturno." In alchemy, Saturn represents lead and the "Nigredo" (blackening) stage, the first step of decomposition.</note>, who keeps it for forty days. Afterward, he takes off his **velvet doublet** <note>original: "Sammetwammst."</note> and gives it to his second chamberlain, **Jupiter** <note>Jupiter represents tin and a transition stage where the matter begins to turn from black to gray or white.</note>, who has it in custody for twenty days. This one gives it to the **Moon** <note>original: "Mond." Representing the "Albedo" stage and the metal silver.</note>, and there the king is in his pure, snow-white shirt.”

The forty days are the time of one **rotation** <term>Rotation: A cyclical process of heating, evaporation, and condensation within a sealed vessel to purify the substance.</term>, and by Saturn, as the highest planet that stands furthest from the Sun, the first rotation is indicated; by Jupiter the second, and by the Moon the third, where the tincture upon silver arrives, and that is the snow-white shirt of the king. The twenty days with Jupiter mean nothing and are only for concealment. But the Moon indicates the white tincture, for otherwise the Moon could not be placed in such steps according to any astronomical division. Further:

> “As soon as the white shirt is there, four of the planets can rejoice, and enjoy great wealth, if they wish,”

because the four metals that stand below silver <note>These are traditionally Lead (Saturn), Tin (Jupiter), Iron (Mars), and Copper (Venus).</note> can be transformed into silver if one wishes to use the tincture for that purpose; but it is more advantageous to leave the **mass** <note>original: "Massa."</note> until the **gold tincture** <term>Gold Tincture: The final Red Tincture or Philosopher's Stone, which transmutes metals into gold and serves as a universal medicine.</term>, therefore he says further: 

> “But in such a way they only possess half the kingdom, and they would do better to wait for the complete outcome, so that they [may be crowned] with the royal crown of their lord...”

<meta>The catchword "krö" indicates the next page begins with "krönen" (to crown).</meta>

The author argues that while this stage allows for the creation of silver, the alchemist should continue the work until the final gold tincture is achieved.</summary>

# The Preparation of the Stone. 123

"...can be crowned, that is, so that they can become gold. He also says: 'During this time, no physician, nor anyone else, should approach.'" Here he indicates that before the **white tincture** <term>White Tincture: The stage of the alchemical work (Albedo) that transmutes metals into silver; it is the precursor to the final Red Tincture or "Universal Medicine."</term>, one does not yet possess the **universal medicine**.

### § 10.

This is the **riddle of Bernhard** <note>Bernhardus Trevisanus (1406–1490), a celebrated Italian alchemist whose "Parable of the Fountain" is a cornerstone of hermetic literature.</note>, over which a thousand seekers for every one successful person have broken their heads in vain. The subsequent work he has described beautifully, and provided much instruction therein; but of the first preparation and preliminary work, he gave none at all. Consequently, nothing was said to the inexperienced person by this, and whoever took this **Bernhard’s fountain** <note>original: "Fontina Bernhardi."</note> for the entire process of the Stone was deceived. But whoever held it to be of no account was also deceived; for a **sophist** <term>Sophist: In the alchemical tradition, a "puffer" or false chemist who relies on clever arguments and superficial chemical reactions rather than the true, hidden laws of nature.</term>—who certainly could not apply these things to his **sophistries** <note>original: "Sophistereyen."</note> and yet, in his own opinion, worked on the Stone—must necessarily have imagined that these things were set down merely as a cover for something else.

It remains, therefore, always true that a special **gift of God** was necessary to learn about the **Philosopher’s Stone** from books—namely, a special gift of God in nature, where one must have a skill in natural understanding to comprehend allegorical and symbolic speech. Where this was absent, all searching was in vain, even if it were the most pious person; for many saints, with great and exceptional gifts of the Holy Spirit, searched in vain for this **secret** <note>original: "Heimlichkeit." Also refers to the "Arcanum" or hidden mystery of the work.</note>, of whom the highly enlightened **Christoph Schütz** <note>Christoph Schütz (1689–1750) was a prominent German radical Pietist. The author uses him as an example of a spiritually gifted man who nevertheless failed at physical alchemy.</note> may stand as an example for all; he worked with sophistries in the coal-fire until his death and found nothing. On the other hand, others have had more knowledge of it who were not worthy of the ground they walked upon—of which the author of the book titled ***House of Light*** <note>original: "Haus des Lichts." Likely a reference to the "Domus Lucis," a title used by several alchemical authors, notably associated with the Rosicrucian or hermetic tradition.</note> was one. This

### Basilius.

->§. 11.<-

**Basilius** <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist (likely a pseudonym) credited with the "Twelve Keys," which describe the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> says in the **seventh key**: "If you wish to be a true guardian of your work, then take the **spiritual water** <note>original: "geistliche Wasser."</note> upon which the **Spirit of God** hovered in the beginning,"—namely, the ensouled matter in the preliminary work—"and close before it the gates of the fortress," with the **Tartar-ferment** <term>Tartar: A crystalline crust (potassium bitartrate) found in wine barrels, highly valued by alchemists as a purifying agent or "fire-extinguisher" for volatile substances.</term>, "for the heavenly city will from then on be besieged by earthly enemies, and your **heaven**,"—the matter ensouled with air—"must have firm protection through three bulwarks and walls," which are the three ferments in the **preliminary work**. "When this is accomplished, then kindle the light of wisdom and seek the **lost penny** <note>A biblical reference to Luke 15:8, used here to represent the hidden essence or "seed" of gold within the matter.</note>," in the subsequent work, "and let there be as much illumination as is necessary," namely seven times, which he does not say here, but in the **11th key** he speaks of it under an ingenious and clever figure, saying:

"There was a magnificent man in the East called **Orpheus**,"—the Tartar—"who was very rich. He married his sister **Eurydice**,"—the **saltpeter** <term>Saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, often associated in alchemy with the "universal spirit" or a cooling, crystalline principle.</term>. "But because he received no heirs and prayed for them, a **flying man** came to him,"—**Mercury** <note>In alchemy, Mercury is often depicted as a winged or flying man, representing volatility and the messenger between the spiritual and physical realms.</note>—"who told him that he should take the blood from his right side, and the blood from his wife's left side, and the blood of his parents,"—as the air—"and unite them together," which happens in the **preliminary work**. "Then he should let this composition pass through the sphere of the **seven wise masters**," which are the seven **rota-**

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the transition from the Preliminary Work (Vorarbeit) to the Finishing Work (Nacharbeit). It references the "Turba Philosophorum," a famous assembly of ancient philosophers in alchemical lore.</meta>

# The Preparation of the Stone.

“...and as soft as wax, otherwise they are of no use.” These are the three <term>Rotations</term> <note>Cycles of heating, cooling, and circulation intended to refine the matter.</note> in the <term>Preliminary Work</term> <note>original: "Vorarbeit." The initial phase of purification and preparation of the "First Matter."</note>, in which the triple foundation of the Stone is laid. They are, however, also the entirety of the Preliminary Work: the <term>black lime</term> <note>The residue left after the first stage of heating (calcination).</note>, the <term>white tincture</term> as the <term>white lime</term>, and the <term>red tincture</term> as the <term>red lime</term>. In such matters, the Sages speak only through the <term>science of numbers</term> <note>original: "Zahlenlehre." Alchemical numerology, where proportions and timing are expressed in symbolic digits.</note> and look to the numbers and parts of the Stone; thus, one does not always have to focus on the literal words, for in the Stone one sees no color other than a white salt. By <term>blackness</term> <note>The stage of *nigredo* or putrefaction, where the matter is broken down.</note>, they signify dryness, and also primarily the Preliminary Work, because this resembles the black and dead earth when compared to the <term>Finishing Work</term> <note>original: "Nacharbeit." The final stages of the process, including projection and multiplication.</note>.

> *Aristenus* in the *<term>Turba</term>* <note>The *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts.</note>: “Cook it moist” in the Preliminary Work, “until the hidden nature appears. And when you see such, then moisten it seven times” in the Finishing Work, “with the remaining water.”

> *Pandulphus*: “Take the **white world**,”—the completed matter of the Preliminary Work—“which is the greatest secret, wherein the <term>red tincture</term> <note>The essence capable of transforming metals into gold.</note> is hidden. Moisten it seven times, and from this shall follow a stone of purple color,”—this is the Finishing Work.

> *Belus* in the *Turba*: “Between the **Magnet** and **Iron** there is a great affinity. This affinity and mixing occurs between them generally in one hundred days.” Iron is <term>Saltpeter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate; in alchemy, it often represents a "fiery" salt or the masculine principle.</note>, which is attributed to *Mars*, and is also the **Body** <note>The material substance or "Corpus" of the work.</note> animated with air; the Magnet is the <term>Tartar-Ferment</term> <note>A substance derived from the crusty sediment of wine (potassium bitartrate), used here as a catalyst to "draw" the spirit.</note>. The hundred days signify roughly the time of one Rotation in the Preliminary Work, for too many days never does harm. Further:
> “Take the water, which you have divided into two parts,”—into the Preliminary and Finishing Works—“and place one part with the body, to melt and cook it therewith” in the Preliminary Work: “but keep the other, to cleanse the **Burned One** <note>The residue left after calcination.</note> and its companion. Moisten the stone seven times, and cleanse it, until it
